1464. BENJAMIN JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of August, a shirt, value 6 d.; a bed gown, value 6 d.; two aprons, value 6 d.; five handkerchiefs, value 2 s.; a waistcoat, value 6 d.; a towel, value 6 d.; a chair-cover, value 6 d.; three caps, value 6 d., and two shirt-collars, value 6 d., the goods of Ezekiel Black.
ANN BLACK. I am the wife of Ezekiel Black. On the 3d of August, I left my house in Cross-street, Soho, about six o'clock in the evening, and left some linen on the floor of the back parlour, which is at the end of a long passage leading to the yard - my husband was up stairs - the street door is always open. I did not stay out more than five minutes, and when I came back, my landlady gave me a bundle which contained the things I had left in my apartment.
ANN SCOTT. I am the landlady of the house. I saw the prisoner go out at the street-door, with a bundle under his arm, I followed and came up with him - he went into a stable-yard, which opens with folding gates. I took it from him and let him go. I brought the things back and gave them to the owner. I am certain he is the man. I looked in his face, and let him go in consequence of his age. I did not speak to him.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I am a poor man in great distress.
GUILTY. Aged 68.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18240916-225]
1465. MARY FENTON was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of September, a frock, value 5 s.; four towels, value 2 s.; a cape, value 2 s.; four glasses, value 4 s.; five knives, value 2 s., and seven forks, value 2 s., the goods of Philip Mose.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the prosecution.
PHILLIP MOSE. I am the proprietor of Furnival's-inn The prisoner was in my service, and left me on the 3d or 4th of June. In consequence of something I heard, I went to her lodging with Reed the officer, and found these things.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
MRS. MOSE. I am the wife of the last witness - I know these towels and the frock by the work. This cape was my sister's - she had left it at my house. These towels are my marking - the prisoner left my house in June, and my husband went to her lodgings about a fortnight ago.
WILLIAM FLAXMAN. I am waiter to Mr. Mose; I know these glasses to be my master's - he has about fourteen dozen of them.
JOSEPH REED. I am an officer - I went to apprehend the prisoner, and found these things in her room - she was scouring the floor.
GUILTY.
Recommended to Mercy. Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18240916-226]
1466. SAMUEL GRANT was indicted for stealing, onthe 6th of September, a piece of dowlas, containing nine yards, value 5 s., the goods of William Graham.
WILLIAM INGRAM. I am a licensed Hawker - I had been travelling with my cloth, in a pack from Kensington to Hammersmith on the 6th of September; there were some navigators sitting on the side of the road at their dinner; they told me to let them see a piece of cloth, and the prisoner asked for a piece to look at; he said,
"Let me show it to this woman," and ran across the way with it. I could not run after him, as my pack was loose, and I was afraid the others would take the rest of the things - it was dowlas cloth.
Cross-examined by MR. CARRINGTON. Q. What is your master's name - A. He has no other name but William Graham. There were many other persons there - I am quite sure it was the prisoner - I never charged anybody else with it. I never charged a man of the name of Taylor with it. When I got to the place with the officer I pointed him out.
WILLIAM GRAHAM. I sent William Ingram, my servant, on the 6th of September, to Brentford, and next morning he came to me drunk.
GEORGE HALL. I am an officer, and took the prisoner in charge - the prosecutor pointed him out to me, and said he was the man who had run away with the cloth. The prisoner said
"I am not the man." I said.
"Perhaps you was in company with him; he said,
"I know nothing about it."
Prisoner's Defence. I am not guilty of the crime.
GEORGE NOAH. I was at work at Kensington, near Lord Holland's, with the prisoner, and near one hundred other men. I was sitting down, between twelve and one o'clock, having my dinner; the young man was coming down the foot-path, and one of the men asked him to let us look at his cloth, and he came and handed his cloth to me and another, and gave a bit to a man whom we called Lankey, he is about five feet ten inches high; he took it to show to a woman, and never came back - not even for his money; his money is now in Mr. Wood's hands - I saw the young man give the cloth into his hands - the prisoner was not there, he came a few minutes after.
WILLIAM INGRAM examined by the Jury. Q. Were you intoxicated - A. No, I was not at all - I had drank nothing but a pint of beer at my dinner. I lost a tablecloth at the same time, but I cannot say who took that.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18240916-227]
1467. MICHAEL GAHAGAR was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of September, a pair of trowsers, value 10 s., and a waistcoat, value 4 s., the goods of Michael Mangan.
The prosecutor did not appear.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18240916-228]
1468. MARY ANN GREGORY was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, a cap, value 20 s., the goods of Christopher Quin.
CHRISTOPHER QUIN. I am a seafaring man, but have lost my sight; a woman came into my parlour, at No. 14, Vine-street, St. Martins's, and said she came for some meat; my wife and I were at breakfast.
ELEANOR QUIN. The prisoner came to my house on the 12th of August in the morning, and went out and in several times in the evening: when she was going out she asked if I would left her put a little paint on her face at my glass - she then took a cap - I did not see her take it, but missed it the moment she was gone.
ESTHER HALL. I live at No. 4, Perkins's-rents: I met the prisoner on Wednesday; she had a cap on, but I did not notice it; a young woman came up and said it was Mrs. Quin's cap; she said it was her sister's.
THOMAS ROGERS. I am an officer. I was sent for to Mr. Quin's on the 15th of September, and the prisoner was given to me, charged with stealing a cap; she said if we would go into Perkins's rents, we should find it at a house - we went and could not find it.
Prisoner's Defence. I said I would go and show a cap I had left at Perkins's rents; but it is not Mrs. Quin's.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18240916-229]
1469. WILLIAM FRANCIS was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of September, twenty-two yards of stuff, value 22 s, the goods of John Lloyd.
GODFREY LLOYD. I am shopman to John Lloyd. of Battic-street, Commercial-road; I saw some person pulling at the stuffs which were hanging inside the door; I went to the door, and my brother asked me if there was a purple stuff at the door; I said there was not; I had seen it half on hour previous; I went out and saw the prisoner running down Baltic-street; I ran after him, and he threw down the stuff; my brother picked it up, and brought the officer down; I never lost sight of him. He said he did not take it, but a man gave it to him; I said,
"You are the man who threw it down" - he said,
"It was a man threw it down to me." I did not see him take it, but saw him throw it down.
Prisoner. Q. Was not I singing out Stop thief - A. The people said so, but I did not hear him; there was no person running before him - he went quietly with me, and begged for pardon.
J - NORMAN. I took charge of the prisoner. Mr. John Lloyd asked him who gave him the stuff, and he said he did not know.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. If I had taken it from the door I could have got away with it in that time. I am totally innocent.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18240916-230]
SIXTH DAY, WEDNESDAY, SEPTEMBER 22. OLD COURT.
Middlesex Cases, Second Jury, Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1470. GEORGE IDEN was indicted for stealing, on on the 24th of August, at St. Andrew, Holborn, a gelding, price 15 l., the property of John Green.
JOHN GREEN. I live at Elsham, in Kent, and am a farmer. My gelding was turned out into my field; I saw it on my farm on Monday, the 23d of August, about eighto'clock in the morning - next morning, about six my son gave me information, and I missed it. I came to town in search of it, and saw it that evening at the Ram inn Smithfield. The witness Hunter brought it to Hatton-garden; it is the horse I lost. I never saw the prisoner about my premises.
Cross-examined by MR. ANDREWS. Q. How long had you had it - A. Five or six years. It was in care of the hostler at the Ram; he is not here. We took it to Hatton-garden - it is now in my possession, and is mine. I had not seen it after the afternoon of the 23d.
GEORGE HUNTER. I live in York-street, Castle-street, Saffron-hill, and am servant to Thomas White, a horse-slaughterer, whose premises are in Sharp's-alley - he lives in Coppice-row. On Tuesday morning, the 24th of August, about two o'clock I was in bed at my own house; the watchman called me up - I came down, and found the prisoner at the door, with a gelding, which was afterwards taken to Hatton-garden, and claimed by Green; I had never seen the prisoner before - he asked me to buy it; I asked what part he brought it from; he said out of Kent, from Tunbridge, and that he had another to bring. I asked his name; he said George Iden: I said I would not buy it at that time of the morning, but if he would take it away, and bring it at a seasonable hour, about nine o'clock, or so, I would buy it - he said he had no place to put it in, being a stranger in the neighbourhood; I said I would put it into my master's premises, and give it some hay - he delivered it to me; I put it into my master's stable. He took a bed at a lodging-house next door to me that night, and between four and five o'clock in the afternoon, as I was going to work, I saw him in the custody of Barnley.
Cross-examined. Q. You saw him taken into custody - A. I saw him in custody. He said nothing about the horse; it was removed to the Ram. I saw nothing more of it till between eleven and twelve o'clock that morning, when it was at Hatton-garden. I swear that is the same horse by the marks upon it; there was a small star on the forehead. I had noticed it in the morning - there was a mark on the side of it, also a saddle mark.
COURT. Q. How long have you been in this business - A. About twenty years. I am a pretty good judge of horses.
RICHARD EYARS. I am a watchman - Hunter lives on my beat. On the morning of the 24th I saw the prisoner on the horse, on Saffron-hill; he asked me where Hunter of York-street, lived - I showed him the house, and heard the conversation between them about the horse - Hunter's account is correct. I went down to Barnley, and gave information - Barnley and I went and took him. I am sure he is the man.
JOHN BARNLEY. I am constable of the Liberty of Saffron-hill. On Tuesday morning, the 24th of August, between four and five o'clock, I apprehended the prisoner next door to Hunter's. I neither threatened, nor made him any promise. I asked if the horse was his; he said it was not; I asked who it belonged to - he said to a man, but he did not know his name, nor where he lived - that he met with him on the Tunbridge-road, and he was bringing it to London, to sell it. I took him to White's place: Hunter gave me the horse at White's stables, in his presence, and Green saw it at Hatton-garden; it was then taken to the Ram inn.
Prisoner's Defence. I was employed by another man to sell it; the first time I saw the man was at the Bricklayers Arms, public-house; he had four horses. A coach passed, and two of them broke from the halter. He asked me to go with him, and I did - I left him at Hyde Park-corner, and on Monday morning I met him; he asked me to go with him to fetch a horse that was coming from Tunbridge - I did so, and met a man; he gave me a direction to sell the horse at Hunter's.
JOHN GREEN re-examined. The gelding is worth 15 l. and more; it was young and sound, only eight years old; it was a very serviceable horse, just in its prime.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it low in condition - A. Yes.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 23.
[trialAccount t18240916-231]
London Jury, before Mr. Justice Littledale.
1471. GEORGE LAKER was indicted for feloniously forging the name of J. D. Faulkener, to a certain bond, with intent to defraud.
MESSRS. GURNEY and BOLLAND conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM WOOD. I am inspector of letter carriers at the General Post Office; the prisoner has been a letter carrier for upwards of seven years - he entered into the usual bond, but his sureties having lately desired to withdraw, he gave as substitutes Edward Laker, his father, and John D. Faulkener, jeweller, Newman-street, Oxford-street. On Monday, the 19th of July, he called at the office to say that his bond was duly executed, and wished to be employed again. I went to the solicitor's office, and saw the bond. (looking at it;) it was executed as it is now. In consequence of suspicion Faulkener was sent to, and introduced to the prisoner at the solicitor's office, and Faulkener was asked if he had executed that bond - he said he did not: the prisoner said nothing, and was taken into custody.
PETER DOWLING. I am clerk to Mr. Parkins, solicitor to the Post-office; I received a paper with the names of the sureties from the prisoner, upon which I prepared a bond for execution; the prisoner executed it in my presence, on Saturday, the 17th of July, and stated that as Mr. Faulkener and his father resided at some distance, he would feel obliged if I would allow him to take the bond to them for their signatures; I gave it to him for that purpose, he said he would bring it back as quick as possible; and if I recollect right, he said he would bring it in the evening, and if I was not in the office, he would drop it into the letter-box, and on coming on Monday morning, I found it in the box - our office is in the General Post Office; it appeared to be executed by his father and Faulkener. Woods came in about two o'clock; I asked the prisoner if he had got that bond duly executed - if it had been signed by his father and Faulkener. The bond was then before him; he said he had, and pointed to Faulkener's signature, and said it was done in Faulkener's parlour, in the presence of John Giles. Faulkener was sent for; I asked him in the prisoner's presence if he had signed that bond - he said he had not, nor had he ever seen it. The prisoner stood mute.
THOMAS DAVIS. I am acquainted with J. D. Faulkener,jeweller, of Newman-street, and am acquainted with his handwriting. The signature to this bond is not his writing; he generally signs with two initials, not at full length.
HENRY WHEATLEY. I am a jeweller, and live in Charles-street, Middlesex Hospital, and have dealt with Mr. Faulkener for several years. I do not consider the signature to this bond at all like his writing.
J. D. FAULKENER. I know the prisoner.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. He applied to you to become security for him - A. He did.
Q. I believe you rather led him to understand that you would - A. I believe he went away under that idea, but my wife disapproved of it, in consequence of which I afterwards refused. I said to him,
"I will not sign it, but anything I can do in your favour I will" - that I was very sorry to refuse him, and would do it with pleasure, but for family affairs.
Q. Did you say, that having promised your wife, you could not sign it, but if your name was signed by him you should not object to it - A. I gave him to understand to that effect.
MR. GURNEY. Q. Do you swear positively that you said that - A. I believe I did.
MR. GURNEY. My Lord, though I do not think this a legal Defence, still I think I shall best discharge my duty by not calling on the Jury for a verdict.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18240916-232]
Middlesex Case, before Mr. Justice Littledale.
1471. GEORGE EVANI was indicted for feloniously, and with intent to defraud his creditors, concealing part of his estate and effects, to more than the value of 20 l., by which he was entitled before the Commission of an act of bankruptcy, amounting in value to the sum of 200 l. and upwards.
Messrs. ALLEY, ANDREWS & LAW conducted the prosecution.
MR. CHARLES REEVES, solicitor, produced a Commission of bankruptcy awarded against the prisoner, dated the 15th of March, 1824.
MR. RICHARD COURTINE. I was solicitor to the Commission produced. I have the proceedings under the Commission. I was present on the 18th of March when the Commission was opened. The Commissioners present were James Trebeck, James Seton, and Edward Grosse Smith, Esqs.; I saw them administer the oath to each other. I have a memorandum of their qualification, and saw them subscribe their signatures to the proceedings. William Smith is the petitioning creditor.
JOSIAH SMITH. I am nephew to Mr. William Smith, of King's-street, Snowhill, one of the petitioning creditors under the Commission against the prisoner. I know of transactions between him and the prisoner. (Looking at two bills of exchange for 78 l. and 40 l.) These are drawn by my uncle upon the prisoner, whom I saw accept them. I gave him his account, and he gave the bills in consideration for that account. I know that my uncle furnished him with goods to the amount specified in those bills before they were accepted. I tendered his account to him, and wished for cash, which he could not give, and I drew these bills, which he accepted.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Who was the money due to? - A. My uncle, and his late partner, Mr. Ricardo. The debt was contracted during their partnership. They dissolved partnership after Christmas, 1823. I came into my uncle's service in March, 1819; I was articled to him only. I know that Mr. Ricardo was a partner; he lived in Weston-street, Pentonville. I manage the concern, and have seen their partnership agreement, though not read it. Mr. Ricardo acted as a partner, and was generally there every day for two or three hours. The partnership ceased in the beginning of 1824; I think about February or March; it was advertised in the Gozette. The goods for which the bills were given were sent to Hastings. I saw the prisoner look out about 78 l. worth, the rest were ordered of our traveller.
COURT. Q. How soon after you went into your uncle's service did you see Mr. Ricardo? - A. He became a partner about the beginning of 1823. He generally posted the books, as he knew nothing of the business, which takes some time to learn; - it is manufacturing portable desks, &c. We always sign bills, Smith and Co., which means Mr. Ricardo.
MR. LAW. Q. Did he act as partner? - A. He did; he had the management of the business. He ceased to attend about the beginning of March I think.
Mr. CONSTANTINE. I am solicitor to Mr. Smith. The partnership ceased the beginning of the year by a notice in writing signed by them both. I know that before that they agreed to dissolve. Mr. Ricardo assisted in winding up the books. They instructed me to prepare an advertisement for the Gazette, which I did. They signed a paper empowering me to do so. This was before the issuing of the Commission.
JOSIAH SMITH re-examined. The bills are endorsed by my uncle, Mr. William Smith; they are still in his possession. I do not know whether they have been to any banker. I cannot say why they are all endorsed - they are sometimes endorsed before they are paid away. I cannot say whether they were discounted, or ever out of his possession. I wished to draw at six months for the amount, but he objected, and I drew at two and three months.
The bills in question were here read; they were payable to the drawer's order, and accepted by the prisoner.
ALFRED VIDLER. I am a plumber, and live at Hastings. I know the prisoner - he lived at Hastings, and in in his window were articles of jewellery, china, time-pieces and fancy goods - he managed the business, and his name was written at the side of the door, Evani, from Russia; I have seen him selling goods in his shop; I have known him in that shop, and at another at Hastings for two years, during which time he exhibited the same sort of of goods - he left Hastings on the 23d of February, and the shop was shut up - he has never returned - the shop was not opened before the Commission issued - I had reason to go to his house for Mr. Smith after he left, to enquire respecting money; there was nobody there; he only occupied the shop and two small rooms behind - his apartments were shut up.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How often have you been there - A. Several times; there is only one entrance to his premises, and the door was closed.
COURT. Q. When did you go last - A. I went with Searle the messenger, who broke in - I shewed him the house. I followed him - nobody was there. I first went there about a week after he left Hastings - he lived in the room behind the shop, and slept there. I only know of his leaving in consequence of his not being seen there - he has been absent at times before, but his shop was never closed.
MR. COURTINE. I produce the adjudication under the Commission, and was present when the Commissioner signed it - it is dated 20th March, 1824 (read)
JAMES SEARLE. I am assistant to Charles Cutten, the messenger under the commission. The prisoner having absconded, I stuck up a notice in his premises at Hatings, for him to surrender to the Commissioners. I stuck it over the parlour mantle-piece - Vidler shewed me the shop.
Cross-examined by MR. PLATT. Q. You stuck it in the parlour - A. Yes; the only entrance is through the shop. I took possession on the 22d March, and left on the 31st of May, by order of the assignee. I gave possession to Francis Runder, an assignee, and left the notice stuck up then. I had stuck it up the day I entered. I had broken into the house about ten or eleven o'clock. I made no endeavour to find the prisoner to serve him personally.
The notice was here read, requiring the prisoner to appear on the 27th of March, 3d of April, and 1st of May; also the London Gazette, containing the same notice.
MR. CONSTANTINE. I was present at the first meeting, on the 27th of March; the same Commssionrs attended as opened the commission.
MR. C. REEVES. I produce the proceedings under the Commissioner; I have a memorandum of the proceedings at the second meeting, the 3rd of April, the same Commissioners attended as before, and Mr. Hudson qualified that day in my presence, and has subscribed his qualification; the bankrupt did not surrender on that day. The same Commissioners attended the third meeting on the 1st of May, the bankrupt surrendered on that day and the meeting was adjourned; here is the order of adjournment - (read) adjouring to the 1st of June, the bankrupt not being prepared to disclose his effects.) The bankrupt had subscribed a request for further time at the third meeting. I was at the meeting on the 1st of June; Mr. Goulburn qualified then, and the Bankrupt appeared - (read) -
"F. Runder, one of the assignees, having stated upon oath that he had reason to suspect that property was not accounted for - this meeting is adjourned to the 15th of June."
Q. Now turn to the 15th of June - A. The bankrupt again surrendered, and subscribed his examination on that day, and delivered in accounts which are annexed to the deposition made by him; they contain a balance sheet, and an account of the disposal of his property.
The deposition made by the prisoner was here read, stating that George Evani having appeared before the Committee, on his oath, saith that the book marked A, produced and delivered up by him, together with the goods and things seized by and under the commission, and the effects and books delivered up, together with 2 s., do contain, and are a true disclosure of all his estate and effects, &c., except such as have really been sold or disposed of, and money laid out in the necessary expenses of his family, and hath delivered up all his goods &c., as are now in his custody, power, or possession, except wearing apparel, and has not reserved or concealed any property, books, or papers, with intent to defraud his creditors.
Signed
G. EVANI.
MR. REEVES. Here are his accounts; I have examined them carefully; the articles enumerated in the indictment are not mentioned in any of the accounts, to the best of my knowledge. Mr. Runder, one of the Assignees, had the book marked A.
The balance sheet was here referred to, and contained no account of the property in question.
JAMES SEARLE re-examined. I took possession of the goods at Hastings; there was no watches, lockets, bracelets, ear-rings, rings, brooches, or gold chains; there were some common seals and keys, but not gold ones; there were six or seven neck-laces, three or four of them gold, but no spectacles or eye-glasses, thimbles, nor musical snuff-boxes.
Mr. FRANCIS RUNDER, an assignee under the Commission here executed a release to the estate, giving up all his interest on any dividends, &c., under the same, and was then examined.
MR. RUNDER. I am an acting assignee under the Commission, and was present on the 15th of June, when the prisoner's last examination took place. I had two books in my possession - I do not know how they were marked. They were the same books as the bankrupt produced. I looked over them, to see if there were any small debts which could be recovered. They were produced to me four or five days before the, last meeting, and left at my house. I took them to the meeting, and gave them to my co-assignee, or Mr. Lewis's, the accountant. I did not look over them carefully - I do not know what has become of them.
MARK EDWARD LEWIS. I am an accountant, and was employed by the assignees to make up these books - the bankrupt attended me for that purpose; the accounts were made under his direction, from bills handed to me by the assignees. The books were not produced to me. I saw him produce a book marked A, at the last meeting. The last time I saw it was in Mr. Runder's possession - the bankrupt was asked for them at the examination on the 15th of June, and said Mr. Runder had them. Mr. Runder came without them, but went home and brought, I think, only one - it was handed in with the accounts. I sat within half a yard of the bankrupt - I do not know what became of the books.
MARY HUTCHINSON. I live at No. 5, Upper Eaton-street, Pimlico. Glasspoole is my cousin, and lived in Three Tunn-court, Borough, in April last, with her mother. On the 4th of April, at ten o'clock at night, she came to my father's in a coach, and brought two small boxes, covered with carpet. They were put into a cupboard under the china press, in our front kitchen - she went away; the carpet was sewed round them with pack-thread - one was moved into a closet under the stairs, and the other into a closet in the area. Three of the creditors and a messenger came on the 10th of August and fetched them away. I saw the prisoner three doors from my father's house, in the street, on the 10th of August, when he was taken. Glasspoole was at my father's house that morning, and Evani knocked at the door, which I answered; he asked for Miss Glasspoole - I said if he would walk a few paces from the door I would send her to him, but I said nothing to her - she went out. I did not observe where she went to - she returned in about five minutes. I didnot see Evani again - she staid about half an hour, and then left.
JAMES HUTCHINSON. I am the last witness's brother. These boxes were taken away on the 10th of August. On the 13th, about half-past nine o'clock, I went to the Coach and Horses public-house, Dover-street, Piccadilly; and about half-past ten o'clock the prisoner came in - the officers were in waiting - he said to me,
"Sir, the boxes and property do belong to me."
Q. What boxes did he mean - A. I cannot say that he meant these boxes; they were then in possession of the creditors; I had never seen them in my father's house myself; he said the boxes belonged to him, and if I would give them up he would give me every satisfaction that they belonged to him.
Q. What had you said to him to produce this conversation - A. The boxes had been the cause of much conversation between Glasspool and me. I did not mention her name to him.
Q. Tell us how the conversation began - A. He addressed me nearly in those words: it was merely a plan for his apprehension; I therefore seemed to agree with his proposal; he inclined his head on the table, and said,
"Sir, my head is under your arm;" the officer with a creditor then came in and took him.
MARIA HUTCHINSON. I am the last witness's sister. On the 12th of August, about half-past twelve o'clock at night, when my cousin Glasspole was leaving our house, I saw the prisoner on the opposite side of the way; she had come about half-past eleven o'clock; and when she went out, he was standing opposite the door; she went down by the public-house to go down Arabella-row; he walked very slowly after her while I had the door open, but I pulled it open again, and saw him walking very fast after her, and crossing over: on the following morning, about seven o'clock, Glasspoole came again, and I saw the prisoner walking about for a considerable time, and about eight o'clock he knocked at the door: I was at the foot of the stairs, and heard him ask for Miss Glasspoole, my sister, who opened the door, told him if he would walk a few paces Miss Glasspoole would come to him; we went down and told her; she went to the door, and said,
"Where is the man?" my sister pointed to him, and she went out immediately: I saw the boxes on the night of the 4th of April, but was out when they were brought; I saw them next day in the china press, covered with carpet, and sewn with pack-thread; I saw Searle take the same away: I had moved them from the china press.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you ever see the boxes - A. They were covered with carpet, but I saw through the pack-thread; one was a black wooden box, and the other a hair trunk.
MARTHA HUTCHINSON. I am mother of the last witness. On the 4th of April, Glasspoole my niece brought two boxes to our house; soon after Christmas I was at Mrs. Glasspoole's, Three Tunn-court, Borough, and saw Evani there - and again on the 25th of February at tea; I merely called in for a rest; it was candle-light, and I stumbled over some boxes on the floor (on the 25th of February); he was in the room; Hannah Glasspoole made an observation about them, but he was not then present.
JAMES SEARLE re-examined. On the 10th of August I seized two boxes at Eaton-street, which I now produce; they contain watches, gold chains, lockets, bracelets, earrings, brooches, and other jewellery; I should value them at 1000 l.
MR. JOHN FURNESS, JUN. My father is a jeweller, and a creditor of the prisoner's to the amount of 220 l.; I was present at the sale and delivery of the goods to the prisoner on the 7th of January - here are about 90 l. worth of the same goods here - here are seven gold seals, worth 10 l. cost price - eight gold rings, worth 5 l.; a necklace, value 10 l.; six pearl lockets, value 9 l.; nine pairs of ear-rings, value 14 l.; five gold chains, value 20 l.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. How do you identify them - A. By our private mark, which is on every article we sell; but we have not sold similar articles of this pattern precisely.
ROBERT WEST. I am shopman to Mr. Adams, watch manufacturer, St. John-square. Here is a watch worth full thirty guineas, in this box, which he sold to the prisoner in January - I saw it delivered to him; here is another, worth ten guineas, which was sold to him at the same time - here is another, worth six guineas, and a gold one worth ten guineas. He bought eight.
Cross-examined by MR. PLATT. Q. Do you mean to say your master has no others made in the same way - A. One I know is of a description which he never sold to any one else; it is a very handsome chased dial and case - they all go through my hands; there are many marks about it, and the number, by which I can swear to it, No. 37,240; I swear that watch was sold to him. I do not mean to say I recollect it by the number alone, but by the appearance and number also. I have valued them at the wholesale price. I should take off 15 per cent. for profit.
MR. RUNDER re-examined. Here are four gold necklaces, which I sold to the prisoner; they are worth 22 l., without profit. One was sold on the 3d of January; the others on the 13th of September, and here are three gold seals, value 5 l. 10 s., and four butterfly brooches, value 7 l., sold him on the 3d of January.
JAMES SEARLE re-examined. I seized all this property at Pimlico.
MR. REEVES. I saw the bankrupt sign every sheet of the proceedings under the Commission; he signed the memorandum of the 15th of June.
GUILTY. Aged 36.
Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18240916-233]
London Jury.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1472. WILLIAM MUNK was indicted for willful and corrupt perjury.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
HENRY BREWER produced an affidavit, sworn by the defendant before Mr. Justice Best, in the cause,
"Stackhouse against Foster and Field, last Hilliary term."
CHARLES TOWNSEND. I am an attorney, and know the defendant's writing. The signature to this affidavit is his, I believe. I have frequently seen him write; he was a client of mine.
JOHN OAKLEY. I have the bail piece, in the cause of Stackhouse and Field, and the rule for the allowanceof bail. R. C. Mullett of the Spur Inn, High-street, Borough, is one of those bail.
Cross-examined by Mr. ANDREWS. Q. You have known Mr. Mullett some years. - A. Yes; I never knew him in difficulties.
Mr. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you know him before 1821 - A. Yes. I should think he was not in a situation to borrow small sums of money.
The affidavit was here read, in which the defendant deposed as follows: - That he had personally known R. C. Mullett for several years, and was acquainted with his pecuniary circumstances, and confidently believed him to be generally poor; so much so, that he had, upon repeated occasions, prior to the year, 1821, borrowed of the defendant, various small sums of money; but although repeatedly applied to, invariably failed in making repayments, urgently pressing for further indulgence upon the ground of poverty; and that the defendant had, at the request of the said R. C. Mullett, sometime back, lent him his acceptance for 23, upon a solemn pledge, that the amount should be deposited to meet the bill when at maturity: which the defendant had, however, been compelled to pay: that the amount of the different loans and goods sold him, exceeded 40 l. which had been due since 1820; all of which remained unpaid; and that the defendant had arrested him in Michaelmas Term for the recovery thereof, which action remained unsettled: also, that the defendant had heard, and fully believed, that the said R. C. Mullett was at present indebted to Mrs. Week, of Kennington, between 400 l. and 500 l. which she had repeatedly declared her utter impracticability of obtaining payment and her apprehensions that she should lose the whole."
I RICHARD CHARLES MULLETT. On the 30th of January kept the Spur Inn, in the Borough. I had been an auctioneer, and lived in Blackman-street and King-street, Borough. I tendered myself as bail in the action in question; I have known the defendant about seven years, but have never borrowed money of him at any time whatever, nor did I owe him any money in 1824. He brought an action against me in November last, but it has never been tried; notice of trial has been given and countermanded; it was brought in Surrey, in Michaelmas Term. I have appeared to defendant, but it has never been tried; I certainly do not owe him any such sum.
Q. Had you any bill transactions with him. - A. I drew one bill of 23 l. on him, and 2 s. for the stamp. He came to me at the Spur Inn, where I was sitting, and said he had met with a misfortune and broken my chaise at Bexley, and as it was in price to him before, the best way would be for him to buy it. I had asked him 25 guineas for it, and at last agreed to take his acceptance for 23 l. and that is what this bill was given for, and for no other purpose. I never promised to take it up; on the contrary, it was for the chaise; it was duly paid; it was payable at Sir John Pinhorn 's. I never had any other bill transaction with him whatever. Mrs. Week, of Kennington, is my mother-in-law. I do not owe her 1 s.; I never borrowed any money of her, nor has she ever applied to me for payment of any. This affidavit was read, but I was allowed to justify.
Cross-examined by ANDREWS. Q. Who delivered the chaise to Mr. Munk - A. He came and took it while I was absent. I had bought it of Mr. Ingram. I was not concerned in the bankruptcy against the defendant, except being employed to take an inventory. I swear distinctly that the bill was given for the chaise, and not accepted for my benefit, or for me to take up. I never proposed to settle this, if he stopped the action against me, nor authorised any one to do so.
Q. Did you instruct your attorney to plead a set-off to that action - A. I did for business done. I did not direct him to insert the price of the chaise in that set-off.
CHARLES TOWNSEND re-examined. In March, 1820, I was dining at the Spur-inn with Mr. Mullett, and recollect the defendant coming in, and stating that he had had an accident with the chaise, he had borrowed of Mrs. Mullett's wife during his absence. It was ultimately agreed that Munk should pay a bill of 23 l, and give a gallon of brandy. The bill was drawn and accepted; I got a friend to discount it for Mullett; 2 s. was added for the stamp.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you ever seen the gig - A. Often, when Mullett and when Munk had it. I have acted as attorney for Mullett, and put in a set-off.
Q. I this it - (producing it) A. Yes; there is a charge made for the gig and harness.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Before you delivered that set-off, had he delivered his bill of demand, charging this bill of 23 l. - A. Yes; and we gave that in answer.
COURT. Q. Did you put that in the set-off by consent of your client - A. I think I told him, I intended to do it, as we might be excluded from proving it, unless it was inserted in the set-off.
MR. ANDREWS. Q. Was the set-off made by Mullett's express direction - A. Certainly not. I exercised my own discretion, with his consent.
MRS. SARAH WEEK. I am Mr. Mullett's mother-in-law. He does not owe me 400 or 500 l. or any sum, nor ever did. I never told Munk so. I never had any conversation with him in my life; I never said so to any body, nor declared his impossibility of paying it.
MR. ANDREWS addressed the Jury in behalf of the defendant, and called
JANE LANE. I am the defendants sister, and lived with him until I was married, which is four years ago next November. Mr. Mullett was then in business, as an auctioneer, and lived in King-street, Borough; he was in the habit of borrowing small sums of my brother; it might be from 5 to 20 l. He was in the habit of coming in, and saying,
"I am short of money, can you lend me some?" This may have happened two or three times a year, in the way of business.
MARY ANN COOPER. I am also the defendant's sister, and have lived with him until within the last seven months. He was in the habit of lending Mullett different sums, at different times some years ago; sometimes 5 l. and sometimes 4 l., or it might be more; I suppose it was paid again. One morning, as I was at breakfast, Mr. Mullett came in, and wished Mr. Munk to lend him his name for 23 l. as he was rather pressed for money, and it would be of service to him, as he could immediately get it discounted. My brother lent him his name without the least hesitation. He mentioned 23 l. and said,
"I will make it a few shillings more, because it won't look so much like accommodation." This was in the spring of the year 1820. He said my brother need not give himselfany trouble about it, he would find the money, Mullett was afterwards at our house, and my brother blamed him for not taking up the bill, Mullett wanted to turn it off to a chaise my brother had bought of him. My brother said,
"No; you know you owe me the amount of the chaise in spirits and wine; I will not set the chaise against that" They had a quarrel about it. I knew myself of wine and spirits having been delivered to him. I sent them myself.
Mr. ADOLPHUS. Q. Your sister was present at this conversation - A. No. It happened at my brother's parlour in the Borough. He kept a wine vaults.
CHARLES MONTEIN. I was attorney for the plaintiff Stackhouse. I saw Mr. Mullett when this suit was pending, (about February last.) I believe he called Munk a great scoundrel and a rascal. When Mullett appeared to justify bail, it was settled that the attorney should produce to me proof of Mullett being possessed of the property he stated, and if I was satisfied, he was to justify. I saw Mr. Oakley; he said, I might depend upon it, that Mullett had the property sworn to, and I think he added,
"but I will not say, it is not mortgaged for its value.
Mr. ADOLPHUS. Q. You did not oppose the bail any further - A. I thought it unnecessary as I had two others.
GUILTY.
Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18240916-234]
NEW COURT. (6th Day.)
Middlesex Cases, Fourth Jury, Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1473. WILLIAM PARRY was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of August, a handkerchief, value 3 s., the goods of Richard Attenborough.
WILLIAM HAYES. I live with Mr. Richard Attenborough, pawnbroker, Sun-street. On the 2d of August, about half-past five o'clock in the evening, I observed the prisoner looking at some handkerchiefs which were pinned on a line. I went out, and came back in about a minute; he was then looking at some others, and asked the price of one. I told him 3 s. 6 d., but agreed to take 3 s. for one; and while he was buying it I observed that he did not use his left arm as he did his right - I then took the money, and laid hold of his left arm, and found this handkerchief under his arm, his coat was buttoned. I charged him with stealing it, he said he had found it.
THOMAS VANN. I took him into custody.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that he found the handkerchief on the floor.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18240916-235]
1474. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of September, a printed bound book, value 18 d., the goods of Sarah Durham.
SARAH DURHAM. I live at Stepney; I was in my room adjoining the shop on the 11th of September, and heard the moving of halfpence. I went into the shop, and saw the prisoner with his hand in my till; I said,
"Here is a man robbing me:" he went away - I was much terrified, and made way for him to pass. I followed him two doors, and then could follow him no further: he was pursued, and brought in less than five minutes, and a book was found in his hat, which I had seen that day, and had not sold it.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
J. J. DOWNES. I saw the prisoner caught by a stranger, who gave him into my custody in the shop - I found the property on him.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18240916-236]
1475. GEORGE ELSEY was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of September, four table-cloths, value 5 s., the goods of George Robinson and Edward Robinson.
HENRY MOSS. I am a porter to Messrs. George and Edward Robinson. Last Friday I went to fetch a pitcher of water; on my return I met the prisoner a yard from the door with these table-cloths in his apron. I collared him, and said he had something that did not belong to him. I put him into the passage - he said he was very sorry, and hoped I would forgive him. Another man came in, and wanted a ham bone; I told him we had not one - he said the prisoner was a poor man, and hoped I would be kind to him.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
JOHN SCOTT. I took the prisoner into custody.
GUILTY. Aged 40.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18240916-237]
1476. WILLIAM CARTER, EDWARD HARRIS, and CHARLES JONES were indicted for stealing, on the 11th of August two pairs of shoes, value 3 s., the goods of George Ferguson.
GEORGE FERGUSON. I am a haberdasher, and live in Ratcliff-highway. I had some shoes out at my door on the 11th of August, and next morning I missed two pair.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
JAMES FOGG. I am a Thames police-officer; I saw the three prisoners watching about Mr. Ferguson's shop; I stood opposite them on the other side of the way; I was called aside by a gentleman, and while I turned my head, I missed them, and saw them going up the Highway; Carter was putting one of the shoes under his jacket; I followed till I got some person to assist in taking them; I stopped Carter and found one shoe on him, and at the watch-house I found three shoes on Harris; I asked Carter where he got the shoe, he said his father gave it him, he was a shoe-maker, in Rosemary-lane; I said I would go to his father; be then said he had no father, and that Jones gave him the shoe; Jones denied it at first, but at last said he had, and would not have taken them if he had not been hungry.
CARTER'S Defence. We were standing round a pump, and Jones came and asked if there was any ladle; Jones was not near us at the time.
CARTER - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Confined Three Months.
HARRIS - GUILTY. Aged 15.
Confined Two Months.
JONES - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Whipped, and discharged.

1411. LYDIA DOWLING, was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of September, twenty yards of printed cotton, value 20 s., the goods of William May Lander and Thomas Dingley, privately in their shop.
WILLIAM MAY LANDER. I am in partnership with Thomas Dingley. On the 14th of September, about half-past nine o'clock at night, I saw the prisoner in our shop, she looked at some things, and bought half a yard of plaid, for which she paid 8 d., she then left the shop, I looked at the window and saw something under her arm: I went out and told her she had something she ought not to have; she said she had got it, and if I would say nothing about it she would give it up to me, but she did not; I took her to Queen-square, and the print was taken from her there; she is, I believe, a respectable woman.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18240916-239]
1478. JOHN COOK was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of August, a bonnet, value 16 s. 6 d, the goods of Mary Balaam, widow.
MARY BALAAM. I live at Bull Cross. My little girl was sent on an errand with her bonnet on on the 16th of August, about four o'clock, and she came home without it.
PHILLIS BALAAM. I am thirteen years of age. On Saturday, the 16th of August, I was playing in a barn with some children, and pulled off my bonnet, and hung it on a nail in the barn: I went on Sunday night to look for it, and it was gone; I was going on an errand on Monday morning, and met a woman with it.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
JAMES CLARKS. I live at Mr. Cole's, Bull Cross. This little girl lives servant there; my master sent me after this man, when she said she had lost her bonnet; I pursued him three miles, and found him with a bonnet and a gown; he said he had bought the bonnet, and he would not deliver it up till he had found the man he bought it of, who lived at Mr. Bottomly's. This was about nine o'clock on Monday morning.
JOHN MEAD. I am an officer. I apprehended the prisoner. The bonnet was given by me to Clarke before the Magistrates.
Prisoner's Defence. A man came up to me as I was going to Waltham Abbey; he goes by the name of Baker or John Russel - my wife said he had a bonnet to sell - if I would buy it she would be obliged to me.
JOHN MEAD. I made enquiry for the person he said he had bought it of, but could not find any such person - the prisoner has no child of an age to wear this bonnet.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
[trialAccount t18240916-240]
1479. JOHN COOK was again indicted for stealing, on the 13th of August, a gown value 1 s.; six aprons, value 4 s.; and six towels, value 2 s., the goods of Henry Hill.
CATHERINE HILL. My husband lives at Bull-cross. I hung out a gown to dry on Thursday, the 12th of August; we missed it next morning with some other things.
(Gown produced and sworn to.)
JAMES CLARKE. I took the prisoner with the gown on his back; he wanted to leave his bundle behind him at the lodge at Mr. Bottomly's; but as we were coming back I met some gentlemen, who asked me if he had a bundle. I went back and got the bundle, which the prisoner afterwards opened - the gown was in it - he said he had swapped away a jacket for it.
JOHN MEAD. The prisoner said he bought the gown of the same man he bought the bonnet of.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18240916-241]
Before Mr. Recorder.
1480. WILLIAM ROLFE was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of August, at Littleton, a cow, price 3 l., the property of Mary Rutter, spinster.
JAMES WARREN. I am in the employ of Mary Rutter; she is a single woman, and lives at Littleton, near Chertsey. She had a large red cow, with wheel horns; she was full of milk. I had been suckling her between six and seven o'clock in the evening of the 5th of August; she then went into the field, and I missed her at five the next morning. There is a large hedge round the field. The gate was closed. I saw the cow again on the Saturday evening at Mr. Lansdown's.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was this a good milch cow - A. Yes; she was worth 5 l. or 6 l. to us, but would not fetch more than 3 l. 10 s. in the market. The boy who has the care of the field generally pins the gate; he is not here. I did not put her into the field, nor go in again that night. I have known the prisoner some time, he is a man who sells cows and calves; we have had dealings in that line together. I have known the cow and had the care of her for fourteen years.
THOMAS CURTIS. I am hostler at the Coach and Horses, public-house, on the Hounslow-road. I saw the prisoner at our door with a red cow, with wheel horns on the 5th of August, about twelve o'clock at night. Our house is five or six miles from Littleton. He asked me if there were any Chertsey people there; I said there was Blake and Turner there; they are butchers of Chertsey. He said,
"I believe I shall not stop, I shall go gently on - good night."
Cross-examined. Q. Did he say, tell Blake and Turner I am gone - No, he did not tell me to tell them, or not to tell them. I went into the house about twenty minutes after, and told them what he said. He did not say what road he was going, but he went up towards London. He had got the cow in a string; he was not more than two minutes with me. I saw that it was a red cow, with wheel horns - there are many cows of that description. I have known him twelve years. He always had an honest character.
GEORGE BLAKE. I am a butcher of Chertsey. I was at the Coach and Horses on the 5th of August with Turner. I left about half an hour after the hostler spoke to me, a little after twelve o'clock. When I got to Smithfield in the morning I saw a cow there, about five o'clock, and the prisoner near it; it was a red cow, with wheel horns, and a large udder of milk. The prisoner came and pocked me in the pole. I said
"What have you got here." He replied,
"A great red cow; I have sold her to Mr. Lansdon for 3 l. 5 s." I asked him where he got it. He said he bought it of Mr. Dover, of Napp-hill, which is about six miles on the other side of Chertsey, for 3 l. He said hehad bought her a week, ago but had not brought her up till yesterday. Napp-hill is sixteen or seventeen miles from the Coach and Horses. When I got back to Chertsey, hearing that Mrs. Rutter had lost a cow, I had him taken up.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he know that you was a Chertsey man - A. Yes, he told me at once who the person was to whom he sold the cow - he knew I was going back to Chertsey. I never bought a horse in my life that was claimed afterwards. I have known Rolfe twelve years; he is a jobber in calves and cows. I never heard but that he was a very honest boy. I took the prisoner at Chertsey on the same day we left Smithfield; on Friday, the 6th of August.
WILLIAM TURNER. I am a butcher at Chertsey. I was at the Coach and Horses with Mr. Blake, and then went on to Smithfield. We did not overtake any cow on the road. I saw the prisoner at Smithfield; he said.
"Good morning to you - have you got your cart here?" I said, Yes. He said,
"I shall stand a chance to ride home" - I said
"It will be a poor chance, I am afraid, for if we have luck we shall buy calves enough." I saw the cow go down to the slaughter-house with two drovers, and I ordered Mr. Lansdon's man not to kill it, for I thought it was stolen. I went to the prisoner, and said,
"That is a large cow you have brought up, she has plenty of milk;" he said her udder is only corey; she has not been suckled for a fortnight - that he had bought her of Mr. Dover, for 3 l. 6 s., and had had her at home for a week.
Cross-examined. Q. How long did you stop in the house after Curtis told you what he did - A. About half an hour - the public-house is about nine miles from Hyde Park-corner, and three from Brentford - we trotted on to Brentford, and then walked the rest of the way. I saw the cow and the prisoner in the market. I did not see him pay 15 s., to anybody. I saw him taken to prison by a man of the name of Purser.
THOMAS LANSDON. I am a sausage maker. I purchased a cow of the prisoner on the 6th of August, about five or six o'clock - Mr. Warren took away the same cow on Saturday evening; I gave 3 l. 5 s. for it; it was not worth more - I should not have bought her if I had noticed that she was full of milk. I have known the prisoner six or seven years, and had no objection to buy cows of him. I have never heard anything against him.
Three witnesses gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 18.
[trialAccount t18240916-242]
1418. DAVID BERRY was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of August, a carpenter's plough, value 5 s., the goods of John Hart.
JOHN HART. I am a carpenter. I had a plough stolen from a new house which I was building, in Hertford-road, Hackney; I had lent it some time before to Powel. I saw it again at the office on the 7th or 8th of August. The prisoner had been in my employ, but not at that time.
JOSEPH CARTER. I am apprentice to Mr. Hart. His plough was at the building, being used by one of the men; I saw it their on Monday morning, the 2d of August; it was missed after dinner, when the men came to work. I saw the prisoner at the lower part of the house that morning, between eleven and twelve o'clock; he was known to the workmen. I saw the plough at the pawnbroker's on the Wednesday following.
JOSEPH POWELL. I was in Mr. Hart's employ. I had used this plough in the morning; we went to dinner at twelve o'clock; I left the plough up stairs. We returned at one, but I did not miss it till about three; I saw it again at the office, and knew it.
WILLIAM HENRY MILLS. I am a pawnbroker, and live with Mr. Hewit, of Kingsland-road. I took in this plough, which was afterwards claimed by Mr. Hart, on Monday, the 2d of August; about twelve o'clock. The house from which it was said to be taken, is about half a mile from our shop. I am quite certain it was brought by the prisoner; I lent him 3 s. on it.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. Q. Do you recollect my dress - A. A pair of blue trowsers, black waistcoat, and fustian jacket. He was in the same dress when at the office.
Prisoner to JOSEPH CARTER. Q. On the day you saw me at the building what clothes had I on - A. The same dress as the pawnbroker has described, only he had a blue coat over his fustian jacket.
GEORGE SMITH. The prisoner was brought to the watch-house on Thursday night - he was dressed to the best of my recollection as the pawnbroker has described.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been talking to the carpenter at the building, but I had a different dress to what has been stated.
GUILTY. Aged 45.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Fined One Shilling and Discharged.

1482. THOMAS BAINBRIDGE was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of July, 8 lbs. of cheese, value 4 s., the goods of William Geary.
GEORGE PARTRIDGE. I am servant to Mr. William Geary, of Broad-street, Bloomsbury. On Saturday evening, the 24th of July, the cheese was in the window, which was open. I did not see it taken, but I saw the prisoner about two minutes afterwards, and followed him about fifty yards from the shop, and brought him back with the cheese. I knew it to be my master's.
THOMAS KENNEFIT. I saw the prisoner take the cheese from the corner of the window, and put it on his arm - I thought he was one of Mr. Geary's people. I went and asked if they had sent out any person with cheese.
PHILIP RILEY. I am an officer. I took the prisoner.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. It is my first offence - I took it from distress,
GUILTY. Aged 23.

I saw the prisoner in the shop - I was in the back parlour reading the paper; my wife heard a noise, and when I got into the shop, she hadtaken the brushes from him. I asked how he came to take them, he said through distress, and begged for mercy.

GEORGE SMITH. I took the prisoner to the watch-house, and found on him 3 s. 3 d. I went with Mr. Halliday, and found he had a good character from his master.

1484. MARY BURKE was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of August, twelve yards of muslin, value 15 s., the goods of John Noble.
JOHN NOBLE. I am a linen-draper, and live in Brewer-street, Somers-town. On the 27th of August, about twelve o'clock I lost twelve yards of muslin - I had just brought them home and marked them. I left them on the counter, and went into the garden - my wife called me back, I came, and found some of my goods were gone. I followed the prisoner, and overtook her about fifty or sixty yards off. I charged her with the robbery; she said she had not got any thing. I told her to come back, and the piece of muslin fell from her in walking - it was one of those I had just brought home. She broke from me, I called Stop thief! and she was taken in my sight.
JAMES FOLKES. I am a milkman. When the prisoner got from Mr. Noble, I stopped her.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. A woman came up and asked if I would have any thing to drink, I said No; she said
"Will you come with me to get a couple of shirts made." I took hold of the article, and this gentleman came and took me. I never denied having it, but gave it him directly.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18240916-246]
1485. MARY ANN BARNES was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of September, a shawl, value 2 s., the goods of Thomas Windsor Allen, and Charles James Maynard.
FRANCIS BYAS. I am in the employ of Thomas Windsor Allen and Charles James Maynard, who live in Clare-street, Clare-market; I had shown this shawl to a customer er about four o'clock in the afternoon, it was taken about eight; it hung about a yard from the counter, the prisoner had got outside the shop; I went out and caught her with it; I had seen her in the shop before, she was talking to a person at the corner of the court, I brought her in and asked her to deliver up what she had stolen, or I would send for an officer, she said,
"No, no, you wont," and gave up the shawl; she had come into the shop to take out a small piece of cotton which had been pawned for 4 d., she appeared to be in liquor.
Property produced and sworn to.)
RICHARD GARDNER. I am a constable - I was sent for to take her.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been out with two persons, and was very much intoxicated.
GUILTY. Aged 57.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18240916-247]
1486. MARY APPLEBY was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of September, a pewter pot, value 9 d., the goods of Charles Freake; and a pewter pot, value 9 d., the goods of Thomas Pugh.
WILLIAM BEST. I keep the George public-house, Queen Anne-street, Cavendish-square. I was informed that the prisoner had taken one of my pots; I went and found her about one hundred and fifty yards from my house, she was going backwards and forwards; I asked her what she had in her apron which was under her arm, she said only her shawl and a piece of baize; I felt round her and hear some pots chink; I delivered her to the constable, who searched her and took two pots from her, with the names of Freake and Pugh on them; she made great resistance.
CHARLES FREAKE. I keep the Turk's-head, Harley-street; this woman was brought to my house; I had seen her about the neighbourhood before, but she had not been at my house that day; I do not know where she had taken my pot from; I believe it had been sent out with some ale at noon.
THOMAS SMART. I took the pots from the prisoner, they were in a pocket behind her, not by the side of her.
THOMAS BUCKRIDGE. I am a constable - I found these two large pockets on her, one before and one behind.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was in great distress.
GUILTY. Aged 64.
Confined for One Year.
[trialAccount t18240916-248]
1487. JOHN SPARKES was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of September, eight copper plates, value 20 s., the goods of John Mackey Langham, his master.
JOHN MACKEY LANGHAM. I am a stationer, and live in Red-lion-street, Holborn; I missed nine copper plates on the 8th of September; the prisoner had been at work for me, but was not then; I made enquiries, and heard the prisoner had pledged some things with Mr. Brown, at the corner of Neville-court. I went there and found two of the plates; I suppose them worth about half a crown a piece; I have seen six others since; he had frequently been in my house; they were kept in the work room at the top of the house, he worked in that room.
JOHN GRINDLEY. I am in the employ of Mr. Townley, Russell-street, Covent-garden; I have six copper plates, two of them were pledged on the 6th of September, two of the 7th, and two on the 8th; we advanced 2 s. on each of them, in the name of John Sparkes; I believe they were brought by the prisoner, but I cannot be certain.
RICHARD FAIRHAM. I am in the employ of Mr. Brown, a pawn broker. I took in two copper plates on the 6th of September, for 1 s. 6 d.; I am quite positive they were pawned by the prisoner, in the name of William Spicer.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I throw myself on the mercy of the Court, I did not do it with a felonious intention.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Confined for Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18240916-249]
1488. JOSEPH RICE was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of September, a handkerchief, value 5 s., the goods of George Viney, from his person.
GEORGE VINEY. I live in Parliament-street, and am night constable of Westminster-bridge. Last Sunday week, in the evening, as I was returning from a place ofworship with my mother, my handkerchief was in my right hand coat pocket, I felt the coat lifted up, and turned round and saw the prisoner with it in his hand - I stumbled in turning, and he gave it to another boy about ten yards from him, who immediately ran off with it. I searched for it, but have not seen it since. A man knocked the prisoner back into my arms, as he was running away - there was no other person that could have taken it.
Prisoner's Defence. I never had it in my hand - it was another boy took it and gave it to another boy - he was not dressed at all like me.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18240916-250]
1473. JOSEPH BAXTER was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of September, a pair of shoes, value 2 s., the goods of George Wood.
GEORGE WOOD. I am a shoemaker, and live in Castle-street, St. George's. On Saturday morning, about nine o'clock, I saw the prisoner go into my shop, and take a pair of shoes. I was on the opposite side of the way, talking to a neighbour; he had put them under his jacket, and was helping himself to more - I went in and took him with another pair in his hand, which he dropped.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was in distress.
GUILTY. Aged 52.
Confined One Month.

1474. GEORGE WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of September, two shirts, value 6 s.; two pair of stockings, value 2 s.; a handkerchief, value 6 d., and an apron, value 6 d., the goods of Ann Nichols, widow, from the person of Edward Copeland .
ANN NICHOLS. I live in the Hampstead-road, and am a laundress. I was going with a child on the 18th of September to take some linen home; the child had the bundle, and was close to me. I was knocking at the door of Mr. Cooke, in Little Russel-street, opposite the church, when the child had the bundle snatched from him which contained Mr. Cooke's things - he ran and cried Stop thief! I did not get sight of the person he was following; but he was stopped, and I saw him at the watch-house: the bundle was picked up and put into my hand before I get to the watch-house; it was the bundle the little boy was carrying for me.
EDWARD COPELAND. I am thirteen years of age. I was with my grandmother taking some clothes home, last Saturday week - they were tied up in a bundle, which I had in my hand; we were near Bloomsbury-chuch when a person came and snatched them from me. I saw who it was, and ran after him crying Stop thief! I am sure I did not lose sight of him, till he was taken - the prisoner is the man; he threw the bundle behind him while running; it was picked up and given to my grandmother.
JAMES OXLEY. I am a gentleman's servant. I was at the corner of Bury-street, about eight o'clock that evening, and saw the prisoner with a bundle under his arm come out of Little Russel-street; he ran, and as he turned into another street he threw it down. I pursued, but the watchman stopped him before I came up - I never lost sight of him.
EDWARD DECASTER. I am constable of the night. The prisoner was delivered into my custody; the prosecutrix described the things before the bundle was opened. I found it all correct.
GEORGE FORDYCE. I am a watchman. I heard the rattle sprung about eight o'clock, and a cry of Stop thief! I saw the prisoner running first. I overtook him; this little boy came up and said
"That is the man who took my bundle."
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I had been into Oxford-street, and was rather late. I was running when this man stopped me.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Life.

1491. JAMES BOWLER was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of July, 48 lbs. of lead, value 8 s., belonging to Thomas Steele, Esq. and fixed to his dwelling-house; and CORNELIUS QUINLAND was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen.
FREDERICK WILLIAM TABER. I am a builder, and live in Old Cavendish-street. I know the house inhabited by Mr. Steele - I had repaired it for Mr. Johnson, who sold it to Colonel Steele about two years ago. The prisoner Bowler was at work for me, and had been so about three weeks as a bricklayer - I gave him no orders to do any work at Mr. Steele's. On the morning of the 20th of July I heard Mr. Steele's house had been robbed of some lead; Bowler was at that time at work for me at No. 14. Mr. Steele's is No. 17; there is a communication from one to the other over the roof - I said nothing to Bowler, but went up to the top of No. 14, and saw the lead had been stolen from No. 17; it had been cut, and partly torn from a dormer flap - it appeared to have been done with a trowel. I got an officer, and charged Bowler and Welch, his labourer, with having stolen it. Bowler called me aside - I told him it would be better for him to tell what he knew. In consequence of what he said we found the lead at the house of Quinland in the afternoon of that day, or the day following; Bowler was with us, and we told Quinland that we came for some lead which had been stolen; he keeps an old iron shop in Oxford-buildings, Oxford-street; we found a number of pieces of lead under the counter, and among others three pieces of about 48 lbs.; we asked Quinland where he got it; he said he bought it, and mentioned what he gave for it; we then took the lead to Mr. Steele's house, and it fitted the dormer exactly. I have not the least doubt but it had been stolen from there - the edges were quite fresh, and it must have been lately taken away.
WILLIAM BALLARD. I am an officer - I apprehended Bowler between twelve and one o'clock; he denied the charge most positively - I afterwards went to Quinland - Bowler went before us, and went into the house; I said we had a search-warrant for some lead that had been sold to him by Bowler, pointing out Bowler; he said he had bought some lead, and pointed to where it was; there was a large quantity of lead; we selected the pieces which fitted the dormer - it fitted exactly, and appeared to me to be taken from it, the edges were quite fresh cut.
JOHN WALES. I am an officer - I stood outside Quinland'shouse with Bowler and Ballard - Quinland was told that we had come to look for some lead which had been sold by Bowler; he said he had it at the back of the counter. I saw the lead fitted, the edges appeared quite fresh. Quinland said he had bought one parcel, and his wife the other, and gave at the rate of 14 s. per cwt. I think he stated the date, but I do not recollect it. I asked if he had any book for lead he bought - he said No; he was taken to the office and admitted to bail.
Quinland put in a written defence, stating that he was a young beginner in business, and bought the lead openly, and gave it up without reserve, when it was asked for.
BOWLER - GUILTY. Aged 31.
Confined One Year and Whipped.
QUINLAND - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18240916-253]
1492. LOUIS FRANCISCO was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of July, a watch, value 18 s., the goods of Thomas Farrar.
EDGAR HISCOCK. I am an apprentice to Mr. Thomas Farrar salesman, High-street, Shadwell. On the 17th of July the prisoner came into our shop with a woman between five and six o'clock in the evening, he went behind the counter, where some watches were hanging. I was in the parlour, but could see what was done in the shop; he said he wanted a silver watch - I showed him one, and he did not like it. I showed him three others - he at length fixed upon one for 3 l.; he offered me a farthing for a sovereign, and then said he would go to his shipmates to give him money to purchase it. I did not miss the watch till about two hours after he was gone - it had been on the counter with some others for him to look at - they were then hung on a nail behind the counter as they were before. I have seen it since at the office, and am certain it was one of those I showed him - there was no person behind the counter - we had not one customer in the shop from the time the prisoner left till the watch was missed; I saw him about ten minutes after at a tobacco shop opposite us, but he did not come to our shop again.
FREDERICK NICHOLSON. I am a pawnbroker - I have a watch which was pawned by the prisoner about five o'clock in the afternoon of the 17th of July, for 10 s.
CHARLES TICKETT. I am a constable. I apprehended the prisoner about nine o'clock on Saturday evening, the 17th of September, in a pawnbroker's shop; I searched him, and then let him go. Mr. Farrar came up and said,
"Have you got that man in charge." I then took him again, and on searching him I found a silk purse and a farthing, and a row of cornelian beads. He was a little in liquor, but not much; he knew perfectly well what he was about.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18240916-254]
1493. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of September, a jacket, value 14 s.; and a handkerchief, value 5 s., the goods of William Pickett.
WILLIAM PICKETT. I am a carpenter, and live in Eastbourne-terrace. I had a jacket stolen from me the day before yesterday. I was at work under a gateway in Sydney-street, Stepney. I had gone out to an adjoining house, and on my return my jacket was gone. My handkerchief was in my hat, which was lying by the jacket. I received some information, and laid hold of the prisoner in an adjoining field; I charged him with stealing the jacket and handkerchief - he said he had done no such thing. I took him to the watchhouse. As soon as he saw me in the field, he dropped the articles, and went on. When I saw him first, he was rolling them up, and then ran away. I am quite sure he is the person. The field is about a quarter of a mile from the place where I was at work.
SARAH EVERETT. I live at Stepney. I saw the prisoner come out from the gateway with the jacket under his arm. I went to Pickett, and asked if he had lost a jacket. He came and said it was gone, and then pursued him.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming from one gang of bricklayers, and was going to another - this man was running, and he took hold of me, saying, I was the man. I said,
"What do you want; I will go with you whereever you wish."
GUILTY. Aged 42.
Confined Three Months.

Middlesex Cases, Third Jury, Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1494. JAMES STEVENS was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of September, ten shillings, the money of Samuel Gould, his master.
SAMUEL GOULD. I am a calico-printer. The prisoner has been in my employ from a child. In the course of the last three or four weeks my counting-house was entered by a key, and on Sunday night last I deposited 10 s. there, in a particular situation, having previously marked them; they were put into a desk, which was not locked.
GEORGE GOULD. I am the son of the prosecutor. I sat up to watch the counting-house in an adjoining room, and about six o'clock on Monday morning I heard somebody walking to the door, which was opened with a key. I looked through the key-hole, and saw the prisoner go into the counting-house; he remained there five minutes, and when he came out I stopped him, and found 10 s. on him, which I gave to the constable the same morning. He had had a good character previous to this.
WILLIAM STILWARD. I am an officer. Gould gave me ten shillings, which I have here. I took the prisoner into custody.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury. - Confined 1 Year.
