1495. WALTER NEARY and WILLIAM NEARY were indicted for stealing, on the 6th of August, 100 lbs. of tea, value 26 l.; and a chest, value 1 s., the goods of John North.
JOSEPH TOWNSEND. I am in the service of John North, of Windsor. On the 6th of August I was driving my waggon in Long Acre, the prisoner, Walter Neary, came and said Mr. Bloomfield had sent him for the tea. I let him get into the waggon, and take a chest of tea out, which he gave to the prisoner William. They then went away with it. I went to Covent-garden, and the man in the waggon showed me Mr. Greave's shop, and then he turned and ran away.
Cross-examined by MR. LAW. Q. The persons were strangers to you - A. Yes; I saw the old man last Wednesdayin Newgate. They were not in my company more than half a minute. The tea was taken by one of them, and they were off.
JAMES FANGE. I am in the service of Mr. Bloomfield, who keeps the Rose Inn, Fleet-market. I saw the waggon loaded, and the chest of tea was in it. I know nothing more.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18240916-257]
1496. CHARLES STEVENS was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of July, one thousand eight hundred yards of Irish linen, value 180 l., the property of John James Malcott Richardson and Charles Richardson, his masters; and WILLIAM BOWEN and CHARLES SHILLITOE were indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
MR. BOLLAND, conducted the prosecution.
MR. JOHN JAMES MALLCOTT RICHARDSON. I am in partnership with my brother Charles; we are linen-drapers, and live at No. 78, Oxford-street, and carry on business in the names of Malcott and James. The prisoner Stevens came to engage himself to us as shopman in January last; I was in want of a young man, and upon his representation of his having lived two years in the service of my other partner. (Mr. Williams,) whom we took into partnership at Christmas last, at No. 211, (but who has nothing to do with the business at No. 78), we took him into our service, and he waited upon the customers in the usual way in the shop. In consequence of his representing that he had a large connexion, his father having been fourteen years in the service of Lord Camden, I allowed him to take out goods for those specific individuals whom he named; the Irish linen in question was bought for a customer whom he said he had of the name of Watkins and Thompson, on the 29th of July.
ARTHUR RICHARDSON. I live in my brother's house. This entry of the 29th of July is mine - (reads) -
"928 yards Irish, at 2 s. 1 d. a yard; 466 yards at 2 s. 2 d.; and 41 l yards at 2 s. 6 d;" these goods were ordered by Stevens for Watkins and Thompson - he told me he had those customers for them: I entered their names in pencil - I put them in a truck myself, and the porter and Stevens went with them.
JOHN JAMES MALLCOTT RICHARDSON re-examined. These other figures in the entry are mine; they are
"6 - 4 - 10," which imports that the 928 yards had been sold to Watkins for 2 s. 6 d.; 466 for 2 s. 4 d.; and the 411 at 2 s. 10 d. to Thompson. In consequence of information, we made enquiries of Stevens. I called him up stairs one Saturday, and asked if he had sold some stockings to Mr. Bowen - he said he had. I asked if he had sold any at less than they had cost - he said No; he had sold every thing to Mr. Bowen at a profit - he stated that all the entries which had been made in our books, to the amount of some thousands of pounds for other persons, were for Bowen, and the Irishes had likewise gone there.
Cross-examined by MR. ANDREWS. Q. Did you make him any promise or threat - A. No; I told him there was something wrong, and I had no doubt we should get at it - I had no positive knowledge of these things at that time.
MR. BOLLAND. Q. Did you know that Bowen lived in Jermyn-street - A. Yes; Mr. Morley, Mr. Andrews, Ballard, the officer, and myself, went there - Bowen was at home. I asked where the Irish was which he had received from Stevens - he said a man of the name of Shillitoe had taken it away that morning, and he did not know where; we had a search warrant: we proceeded to search, and found some other property - we found some Irish, but not that stated in the indictment; when we found these things, Bowen stated that some articles, eight pieces of Irish, and some long cloths that were there; he had not purchased, and we might take them away. A great deal of conversation took place; we then went up stairs, and found some more Irish, and some books in a drawer, under a sort of desk - we asked what they were; he said, know Shillitoe's direction, but his wife could tell if she his little account books, and said he had no secrets to keep. We waited till Mr. Shillito came: Bowen said he did not was at home - he had done business with him for ten years. Shillitoe came home in a horse and cart - he hung up his coat and whip, and was coming into the back parlour; we asked what he had done with the Irish which he had taken that morning - he said
"What do you want to know for" - but at last said he had taken it to the house of White and Greenwell, in Blackfriars-road; we went and found the whole was from our house - the whole one thousand eight hundred and five yards, with my marks upon them were there.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. I think you said it was in January that Stevens came to you - A. Yes; and soon after he was entrusted to dispose of goods, and to enter debits in our book to a very considerable amount - there are persons standing as debtors to a very considerable amount in our books whom I never saw.
MR. BOLLAND. Q. Had you any conversation with Bowen as to where he had met with Stevens - A. Yes; he said at a pawnbroker's - I said it was my opinion he had bought of Stevens, because he thought he could get rid of them at a small advantage; he admitted that to be the case.
MR. BRODRICK. Q. Are there not many persons standing on your books as debtors with whom you never had any communication on the subject - A. Yes; Bowen is our debtor to the amount of 525 l. I never had any communication with him, because I had confidence in Stevens; he had never come to our shop to pay money, but Stevens had paid money on his account, and he had paid ready money through Stevens, in sums of 25 l or 30 l. - the whole perhaps was 200 l. or 300 l. His name appears several times. I never myself went to his house to make enquiries, but it was through the representation of Stevens that goods to that amount were delivered to him; he represented that they had a shipping order - there are other debtors to considerable amounts placed here by the same means - the whole may amount to 1700 l. or 1800 l. I never had communication with them - they were all done by Stevens: the entries are in the ledger among others, there is no mark to point out which were brought by Stevens. I did not say to him
"You have nearly ruined us, we may as well know the worst of it." I said
"You might have ruined us." I went to Mr. Bowen's with the officer and two other gentlemen. I believe the officer went in first with Mr. Andrews, and then Mr. Bowen saidhe had no secrets to keep; we found some linen and long cloth in his back parlour, which he stated he had not purchased; there was no concealment - there were several whole pieces of Irish in a box, and some remnants - the box was locked. Bowen offered every facility in the search. I went up stairs after the officer, and saw the box broken open - I do not know whether Bowen gave him any iron to open it. He did not say that Shillito had left Oxford-street, and gone to Russell-square; he said he did not know where he lived at all; we said we must know, and at last he said he believed near Russell-square - I do not know whether he said he had been in the house or not, but he said he did not know where it was. Linen cannot be bought for the same price on credit as for money - the advance is 1/2 per cent. per month; our purchases were at seven months, which would have been 3 1/2 per cent. - that is the usual discount allowed by the trade. We have two houses, but there is no property common to the two houses. Malcott and James are names which I put up, for reason which I could explain; they are two of my own names. The other house is in the name of J. Williams, only - we have no other house, Bowen's is about ten minutes walk from our house. I never on any occasion authorized Stevens to go and dispose of any goods, without expressing any particular customer, or knowing who the purchaser was to be. I never stated to him that I had bought some black cloth at 15 s. a yard, and desired him to go and dispose of it. We have a day book, which is kept by my brother Charles, I am not aware that any entries are made to Stevens. The goods are put down to the name of the young man who carries them out, as we do not know whether they may all be sold.
Cross-examined by MR. PLATT. Q. Have you any interest in any commission shops - A. No, none whatever. We waited several hours for Shillitoe to come home, and then we told him we came for stolen property - he told us where it was taken, and we went and found it. I do not remember his saying that he did not know them to be stolen; he said he had sold them, but did not say for Bowen. I did not state to Mr. George that Shillitoe had independent property to the amount of 300 l. a year, and I had endeavoured to get him detained, in hopes that sooner that be ied at the Old Bailey he would come to some terms, or anything to that effect. I will positively swear that I did not, neither in presence of Mr. George, nor Mr. Blackman - I believe I did say to some person that I believed Shillitoe had property to the amount of 300 l. a year. I remember Mr. George and Mr. Blackman being at the office, but in my presence nothing was said of the kind. Mr. George is a linen-draper in Oxford-street.
MR. BOLLAND. Q. Have you ever in any one instance debited Stevens - A. No. When we put down goods to a clerk it is only a check upon him. These are the accounts which Stevens told us were all fictitious. Every body I deal with knows me by the name of Richardson - my goods are purchased in the name of Williams and Richardson, and are sent to No. 211, or 78. Our bills of parcels are in the name of Mallcott and James, or Williams and Richardson - all those entries are to my young man Stevens, but it is merely to know who took them out. If I was to send goods to you I should put down the clerk's name, and your name in the day-book.
MR. ANDREWS. Q. As this was entered to the clerk in the first instance, shall I afterwards find it in the ledger - A. No, Sir.
Q. Look at that - A. This is cash received, you will not find the entry to him.
WILLIAM BALLARD. I am an officer. I went with a search warrant to Bowen's, with Mr. Richardson and Mr. Morley; it is a sadler's shop. I saw some of the shopmen, and asked for Mr. Bowen: I went into the parlour with him, and told him I had a warrant to search his house for linen, the others were then following me: he admitted that he had the goods I mentioned, with others. I saw some linen drapery lying about, which we took when the other gentleman came in, we asked what was become of the linen that was taken the day before; Mr. Bowen said that a person of the name of Shillitoe had taken it out, he had taken out seventy pieces: he said he did not know where Shillitoe lived, nor where he had taken them to but he had lent him a horse and cart, and it was his in-, variable rule to bring them home, he expected him home that day: he said he had bought the things in a fair way: I asked when and where he became acquainted with Stevens; he said in Jermyn-street: I said
"Was it not at a pawnbrokers shop," he recollected that it was: he only stated while below, that he did not know where Stevens resided: Bowen then said there might be some things which his wife had taken up stairs, and he supposed she had taken the keys: there was a box which he said he had no objection to my opening; I forced it open, and found some linen in it; in the parlour we found some books, the small book Mr. Bowen produced himself; and said they were Steven's own entries, and he had signed his name to the cash received.
Cross-examined by MR. ALLEY. Q. Then Mr. Bowen conducted himself to you in a fair and honourable way - A. Yes, he said he considered Stevens as a job dealer, that he had been referred by Stevens to a person of the same name in Regent-street, before he dealt with him, but he did not make any enquiries there.
WILLIAM WHITE. I am partner in the house of White and Greenwell, Blackfriar's road, on the morning of the 31st of July, I saw Shillitoe at our house; he came with some samples of Irish linen; I purchased of him to the amount of 161 l. 1 s.; they are here, I bought the goods by sample and they were delivered the next morning.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. These goods came in broad day - A. Yes, I gave three different prices for them, one was 20 d., one 21 d., and one 2 s. a yard; that was a fair price. I have been a linen draper sixteen years; it is common with them to turn stock into money about that time; I have not known Shillitoe more than three or four months, he was sent to me as a job dealer, he made no secrecy, and always dealt on fair terms; he gave me references who gave him the highest character. There is no linen here which I would have given 2 s. 6 d. for, I could have bought it for less; I have some of the same as this fine linen which I gave 2 s. 4 1/4 d. for, on regular Irish credit, from seven to ten months; I produced them as soon as I was called upon; I mean by a regular price, the market price, not from a jobber, there is no regular price exactly. I considered Shillitoe as a jobber, he said he lived in Foley-place, I never went to enquire.
MR. JOHN MORLEY. I am a wholesale hosier, and live in the City; I went with the officer and Richardson to Bowen's; I think the officer went in first; there were several pieces of Irish and some silk; they asked him for the Irish, silk, and different articles, which he had bought of Stevens, he very readily showed the goods, and made no objection; in fact they were about the room, some of them in boxes, and there were some books and papers.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. He gave every information in his power - A. Yes, there was one box or two to which he could not find the key. I did not go up stairs, the goods below were lying in the back parlour; he said he bought them of Stevens, but did not know where he lived. I did not hear any thing about a reference to Stevens, the boot-maker, in Regent-street, he said that was where he was to find him, but he did not know of his living in Oxford-street; there were four men in the shop, and these things lying publicly in the room; I am not certain whether there is a glass door between the shop and the room. Bowen went into the coach with Shillitoe and the officer, I walked with Mr. Richardson; there was a warrant to search the house, but I do not know whether there was one to take them.
WILLIAM BALLARD re-examined. Q. Did you take Shillitoe and Bowen to the office - A. Yes; there was a box at Bowen's,; I got a piece of iron of him to open it.
WILLIAM PAMPLIN. I am a porter, and live in Nevan-court, I do jobs for different persons; I have done a good many jobs for Stevens; the chief part of them was to Mr. Bowen's; once or twice he told me to go to particular places, and then countermanded the order; the last time I went to Bowen's I took a parcel; I cannot tell what it was; it was on the 29th of July, in a truck; I made a memorandum of it at the time. I do not recollect any particular direction on that day; when I went there I had a strap with the name of Malcott and James on it. I should think I could not deliver any goods to Bowen without his seeing it, but I cannot tell; it was a broad strap with the letters inside; when it is pulled off, any body. I should imagine could see the name. I do not know that I ever carried goods before eight o'clock in the morning, or after four in the afternoon; I delivered them in the shop where the men were at work, and sometimes they helped them in and I went away; I was called in from the street sometimes by one, and sometimes by another; Mr. Richardson has seen me three times out of four: Mr. Stevens paid me once or twice; I have been told to go to some other place, and then countermanded in the street, and told to go to Mr. Bowen's.
BOWEN'S Defence. The purchases made of Stevens were without a guilty knowledge. Though I am a sadler it is well known that I have been in the habit of buying and selling goods, by which I got a profit. Stevens first applied to me in March last, and gave me reference to Mr. Stevens, of Regent-street; I went there, and made enquiries, which were satisfactory. I bought the goods at a fair ready money price, and sold them again at a small profit; this went on till the end of July, and up to that time I had no idea but that he was what he represented himself to be, and I was confirmed in it by having sent letters to his uncle's and having answers to them I always considered him to be the agent of manufacturers, or dealers, and as such I dealt with him - if I had not, is it likely I should have carried it on, in the view of workmen and servants, and of everyone entering my house. When I was called upon by the officer I did not attempt to oppose him, but gave him every assistance in my power, and acquainted them that Shillitoe was out, and would return in the afternoon; I gave up the goods, and requested the officer to break upon the boxes and drawers. Should I have done this if I had possessed a knowledge of the theft? rather, should I not of had recourse to every method to have concealed them? The very fact of my purchasing such a quantity of goods will, I think, acquit me of any guilty knowledge of the charge, when I must have known that it would be discovered. I have made advances to Stevens for the purpose of purchasing goods, as will appear by a letter I have, in which he requests an advance to be made. I lent him 50 l., and however in cautions I have been I have not done it with a guilty knowledge. I cannot but lament that the prosecutor did not call upon me earlier, or send to me for the debt which stands upon their books; had they done that a considerable part of the debt would been saved to them.
* STEVENS - GUILTY. Aged 24.
* There were two other indictments against Stevens, for stealing sixty napkins, value 9 l., and twelve tablecloths, value 16 l.; also eighteen hundred yards, of nankeen, value 190 l., the property of the same persons; and charging Bowen and Shillitoe with receiving the same; but no evidence being offered, they were acquitted,
BOWEN - GUILTY. Aged 40.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
SHILLITOE - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18240916-258]
SEVENTH DAY, THURSDAY, SEPTEMBER 23. OLD COURT.
Middlesex Cases, First Jury, Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1407. WILLIAM TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of August, at St. Andrew, Holborn, in the dwelling-house of Samuel Sparrow, his master, twenty rows of pearls, value 80 l., and four pearl ornaments, value 1 l., his property.
ANN SPARROW. I am the wife of Samuel Sparrow who lives in Brook-street, Holborn, in the parish of St. Andrew, and is a pearl stringer, we have a great many pearls, and always put them into an iron chest at night - the chest is sometimes left unlocked. The prisoner was an occasional servant. At the time in question we slept out of town for the benefit of my health, and the prisoner slept there to take care of the property - I have known him six or seven years: he had slept there for three months, for the last two summers. On the 10th of August, immediately after breakfast, I went to the chest to take out the property, and missed a quantity of beads; I had been out all the day before, but on the Thursday previous I had put them all into the awer - the drawer belonged to a smallcabinet, and these drawers are all placed in the iron chest at night. I had slept there from Thursday till Sunday, but not on Monday. On missing them I informed my husband. They were worth 80 l. - we have found some of them. The prisoner lived at No. 1, Paradise-place, Kentish-town, and kept the house.
WILLIAM LEE. I am an officer. I was informed of this, and went to the prisoner's house, No. 1, Paradise-place, Kentish-town. The prosecutor took me there himself, on Tuesday, the 10th of August - I found his wife there, and in a bed-room up stairs, in the closet, I found eleven rows of pearls in a piece of paper, in a pickle jar, concealed behind some rags. I had been fetched to Sparrow's, and found the prisoner there - I asked if he knew any thing of the pearls; he said not: I searched him and found one string of pearls in his shoe. I then told him I had been to his house, and that there was a considerable quantity more lost - he said all the rest were between the rafters and tiles in his wash-house - I went there, and found three ornaments.
MRS. SPARROW. The one set found in his shoe are worth 4 l., cost price.
SAMUEL SPARROW. There are ninety-six pearls in a row; they cost me 1 s. each; they were all mine. I think that without exception, till this occured, the prisoner was the most sober and honest man I ever knew.
Prisoner. I took them on Saturday.
Two witnesses gave the prisoner an excellent character.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 40.
Strongly recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
[trialAccount t18240916-259]
1498. WILLIAM LAWRENCE was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of June, at St. Mary-le-bone, six shirts, value 18 s.; two coats, value 40 s.; a pair of trowsers, value 14 s.; a pair of breeches, value, 1 l.; three waistcoats, value 1 l.; a hat, value 10 s.; a watch, value 2 l.; five sovereigns; a half sovereign, and seven shillings, the property of John Thrower, in the dwelling-house of Thomas Higgins .
JOHN THROWER. I am a journeyman farrier, and lodge at Mr. Thomas Higgin 's, Paddington-street, Mary-le-bone. On Wednesday, the 16th of June, I went out at half-past five o'clock in the morning, and left all this property safe. Some was in my trunk, and some on the dressing-table. I left the prisoner in bed - we occupied the same room. I had been there eleven weeks, and found him there when I came. I returned at half-past six o'clock in the evening, and missed the property. I found him at Windsor on the 19th of August, in bed, about two o'clock in the morning, and asked if he knew me - he said Yes, he thought he did. I said,
"Do you recollect lodging with me at Higgin's?" he said Yes. The constable searched the room, and found a good deal of my property with my name on it. The value of all that I lost was 25 l.; he said he had sold most of it. Smith, the constable, gave the property to Morris in my presence.
MICHAEL MORRIS. I was fetched to Windsor - Smith the constable, gave me this property, which I produce. The prisoner told me that he had sold the watch for 18 s.
JOHN THROWER re-examined. He said, voluntarily, that he had sold the watch on the road for 8 s. All this property is mine - here are my brushes, coat, and apron, and several handkerchiefs; and a hat, with the lining taken out. I have not got half the property back.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 21.
[trialAccount t18240916-260]
1499. GEORGE OLIVER was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of August, at St. John, Clerkenwell, a 5 l. Bank note, the property of Joseph Beckwith, in his dwelling-house, against the statute.
JOSEPH BECKWITH. I live in Wilderness-row, in the parish of St. John, Clerkenwell - the prisoner was my errand boy for five weeks. I keep my money in a box in the work-shop - it is generally locked. On the 30th of August, at eleven o'clock in the morning, I went to the box - it was then locked, and I believe I left it locked. I missed a 5 l. note from it before half-past eight; it was safe at eleven. The box might have been left open in the course of the day - the prisoner assisted in looking for it, but we could not find it - he went home at night. I saw the note afterwards in the possession of Mrs. King, and knew it.
SARAH KING. My husband keeps the Nagg's Head public-house, St. Johns-street, very near to Beckwith's - the prisoner frequently came there. On Monday, the 30th of August, about one or two o'clock, he brought a 5 l. note for change, which I gave him - my husband endorsed the note in my presence - I know his writing. I gave it to the officer (looking at it) this is it - it has his writing on it.
JOHN FORBES. I am an officer. On Wednesday evening, the 1st of September, I apprehended the prisoner coming through Smithfield, riding on an ass. I took him to the watch-house, and asked who the ass belonged to; he said to a man in Turnstile - he afterwards said, voluntarily, that it was his own - that he bought it for 30 s. I found 13 s. on him, and asked him where the rest of the 5 l. was; he said he had bought a saddle and bridle for 16 s. 6 d.; also six trusses of hay, and had paid a month's rent for a stable for the ass - that he had changed the note at King's, and bought the ass with the money - I have the note.
JOSEPH BECKWITH. This note has my writing on it, and is the note I lost.
Prisoner's Defence. I picked the note up in the shop, and did not know who it belonged to.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 13.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
[trialAccount t18240916-261]
1500. WILLIAM DANIELS was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of September, a watch, value 5 l., the goods of Edward Durken, in the dwelling-house of Joseph Currie .
EDWARD DURKEN. I am a watchman of Piccadilly, and lodge with Joseph Currie in Black Lion-court, St. James's. On the 7th of September, about six o'clock in the morning, I went to bed, and hung my watch over the bed head: the prisoner was in bed in the room - he had lodged there for a fortnight; I awoke between eleven and twelve o'clock, and missed it; I pointed him out in the street - he was secured, and 9 s. 9 d. found on him, and the duplicate of my watch was in his fob.
JOHN VIRGO BUCKLAND. I am an officer. On the 7th of September, between twelve and one o'clock, Durken pointed the prisoner out to me at the corner of Black Lion-court; I found 9 s. 9 d., and the duplicate of the watch in his fob.
LAUNCELOT WILD. I am shopman to a pawnbroker, who lives in Broad-street, St. Giles's; the prisoner pawned the watch between eight and nine o'clock in the morning,in the name of Davis, Old Compton-street - it is worth 25 s.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 27.
Of stealing to the value of 39 s. only.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18240916-262]
Before Mr. Justice Littledale.
1501. RICHARD TAYLOR, TIMOTHY TAYLOR, and MARY CLIFFORD, were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of James Perry, about twelve o'clock in the night of the 6th of August, at St. James, Clerkenwell, with intent to steal and burglariously stealing therein, fourteen sheets, value 40 s.; four shifts, value 10 s.; a pair of sheets, value 12 s.; four table-cloths, value 20 s.; fifteen caps, value 15 s.; a frill, value 6 d.; seven handkerchiefs, value 12 s.; a cushion cover, value 1 s.; nine pinafores, value 5 s.; three pillowcases, value 3 s.; five petticoats, value 4 s.; seven gowns, value 20 s.; a habit-shirt, value 1 s.; four aprons, value 2 s.; three pairs of drawers, value 1 s., and a pair of stays, value 1 s., his property.
ELIZABETH PERRY. I am the wife of James Perry; we live in Union-place, Bagnigge Wells-road, in the parish of St. James's, Clerkenwell, and rent the house - nobody else lives in it. On the 6th of August I got up about ten minutes before six o'clock in the morning, and went into the wash-house, which is in the yard, and is not more than a yard from the house; the yard belongs exclusively to us, I was in the wash-house the night before at eleven, and left it locked; the window was put down - I left a basket of clothes there, ready to iron: I found the window open, and the towels which I had left on the top of the basket were thrown all over the wash-house, and the basket emptied, except a very few articles. I looked about, but found none of them - the house door was locked; I missed the articles stated in the indictment - (enumerating them) - they were all in the basket the night before; they were my husband's property, and worth 8 l. or 10 l. at least.
WILLIAM COLTON. I am constable of Islington. On the 6th of August I was applied to, and went with the watchman in search of the prisoners. I found Richard and Timothy Taylor on Saffron-hill about six o'clock in the evening, and took them both to Tunbridge-street watch-house, New-road; and at the corner of Tunbridge-street I observed Timothy Taylor throw something from his pocket into a garden. I took him to the watch-house, locked him up, and brought Richard back with me to this garden, and then told him to go on, and stop at my house till I came there, which he did; he went alone, and waited till I came. I desired the watchman to go and pick something out of the garden, which I pointed out, and it proved to be six duplicates, five of which were for property pawned on the 6th of August; I thought one of them related to the property lost, and went over to Button's the pawnbroker's, to enquire about it. Richard Taylor was with me; the pawnbroker said that Richard Taylor had pawned two whittles that morning - I asked him whose they were; he said they belonged to his landlady - I said I must go and enquire, and took him with me; and as we went along, he said he would tell me something - that he had bought some property at five o'clock that morning of a man (who stands indicted, but is not in custody - he has never been home since.) I found at Taylor's lodging thirty different articles of wearing apparel, which I now produce: I saw the watchman pick up the duplicates.
Prisoner RICHARD TAYLOR. Q. Have I not been in employ up to the time I was taken - A. To the best of my knowledge he has: he works close to me.
SAMUEL GAIGER. I am a watchman, and know Richard and Timothy Taylor. On the 6th of August, about half-past four o'clock in the morning, I saw Timothy Taylor standing near Battle-bridge turnpike: nothing passed between us except saying that it was a fine morning; it was day-light. I am in the habit of seeing him about that time; I was with Colton that evening, and apprehended Richard and Timothy Taylor; and at the corner of Tunbridge-street, Richard Taylor, who was with me, crossed over; Colton was on the other side with Timothy Taylor, who was put into the watch-house, and Richard ordered to go to Colton's house. As we returned, Colton sent me into the garden to pick up a paper which he pointed out; I found it was duplicates, and gave them to him: we then went to Button's the pawnbroker, of Battle-bridge, with Richard, and then to Richard's lodging; he said something to Colton about buying something, and in his room the bundle of property was found.
EDWARD BULLSWORTHY. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Aylesbury-street, Clerkenwell. On the 6th of August, the prisoner Clifford came to my shop, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, and pawned two table-cloths, and two shifts, for 5 s. - she wanted 9 s. - they are worth 7 s. I asked if they were her own - she said they were. I have had them ever since.
JOHN BUTTON. I am apprentice to Daniel Button, a pawnbroker, of Albion-place, Battle-bridge. On the 6th of August, about two or three o'clock in the afternoon Timothy Taylor pawned a silk handkerchief for 1 s. 6 d. it is worth 3 s.
GEORGE HARVEY. I am servant to Mr. Cross, pawnbroker, Turnmill-street, Clerkenwell. On Friday afternoon, the 6th of August, between three and six o'clock, Clifford came to my shop, and pawned two shirts for 4 s., and a shawl and shift for 3 s. 6 d. I asked if they were washing things; she said No, they were her own - she came twice within those house; the value of the whole is about 10 s. 6 d.
ALEXANDER MILLS. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Greek-street, Soho. On the 6th August two gowns were pawned, which are worth 6 s. - it was by neither of prisoners.
WILLIAM JORDAN. I am constable of Clerkenwell. On the 13th of August, at ten o'clock at night, I apprehended Clifford at Bagnigge Wells, and asked if she knew any thing of the robbery right opposite - she denied it. I took her to the watch-house, but found nothing on her, and next morning, as I was taking her before the Magistrate, I neither threatened or promised her; she confessed that she had pawned a number of articles which the Taylors had given her, at Cross and Bullworthy's, and declared that she did not know how they came by them - she did not say what they were.
JAMES SHORE. I live at No. 14, -street, Battle-bridge, and occupy two rooms. Richard Taylor lodgedwith me - he lived in one room, and I in the other. On one Friday morning, in August, about half-past five o'clock, he came and knocked at the street-door, (it was on the day the officer came and found the property) I opened the door, and Richard Taylor stood there; he asked me to leave the door open for a few minutes, for he was coming into my room. I left it open - he was in the street, and had nothing in his hand then: I was not quite dressed. I went to the door again when I was dressed, and he came down and brought a bundle, set it down, and there it remained till the officer came at night and found it in my room. I had been out all day and do not know what was in it; it was candlelight when the officer came. I did not notice the handkerchief it was wrapped in.
Prisoner RICHARD TAYLOR. Q. Was I not in bed on the night of the robbery - A. I cannot say, but he wished me good night, the same as on other night.
JAMES PERRY. I live in Union-place, Bagnigge-wells, and rent the house. The roof of my wash-house joins the wall of the house; the tiling comes against it. There is a passage between the dwelling-house and wash-house, which is covered over by a continuation of the roof of the wash-house; you do not go into the air to get to it. There is a yard behind the house; it all belongs to me. I rent it all of one landlord, and under one lease.
ELIZABETH PERRY. (Looking at the property produced by Colton.) These are all ours, and were stolen from the basket in the wash-house on that night. Here are two petticoats, a handkerchief, four childs' shirts, two pockets, nine pinafores, nine caps, a shirt, a habit-shirt, a frill, and silk handkerchief - that is all the bundle contains. They are worth 1 l. 1 s. 6 d. altogether. The two table-cloths and two shifts pawned at Button's are also ours, and the name is picked out of one of the table-cloths, but the date of the year is on it, by which I know it. The handkerchief pawned at Button's is ours. Here are three shirts and a shift pawned at Croft's. The two gowns pawned at Mills's are also ours. Besides what have been produced, I missed five sheets, two handkerchiefs, five day-caps, four night-gowns, two petticoats, two table-cloths a pair of sheets, and three pairs of drawers, value together 3 l. 4 s. On the day before the robbery two dust men applied at the door to take the dust away - I let them through the house into the yard, and the linen was then drying in the yard. The prisoner. Timothy Taylor, I know to be one of the dustmen. I never pawned anything with Mills.
JAMES PERRY. We always use the wash-house. I never pawned anything at Mills's.
EDWARD BULSWORTHY. One of the duplicates produced is what I gave Clifford.
JOHN BUTTON. One of the duplicates produced is what I gave Timothy Taylor.
GEORGE HARVEY. Two of the duplicates produced are what I gave Clifford.
ALEXANDER MILLS. The duplicate I gave the person is among those produced. It is in the name of Perry.
Prisoner RICHARD TAYLOR to COLTON. Q. Did I not tell you where the gowns were which I had bought - A. I said so - he told me he had bought them of John Barry.
RICHARD TAYLOR 'S Defence. It is customary for me to get up about 4 o'clock in the morning, and as I was going to Battle-bridge, about half-way down Gray's-Inn-lane, a person stopped me, and asked if I wanted to buy any shirts. I asked to see them. He showed me four, and wanted 10 s. for them. He put some more things out of his bundle - petticoats and other things. I said they were of no use to me; he then said he would sell me the whole bundle for 2 l. I said I had but 30 s. I bought them of him for that. I was in bed on the night of the robbery.
TIMOTHY TAYLOR 'S Defence. This man asked me where he could pawn these things. I took him down to this woman's house, and she pawned them.
CLIFFORD'S Defence. On the day in question I went to see Eliza Harris, and while there these two men came in, and asked me to pawn the things. I asked if they were their own? They said Yes; and I took and pawned them in my own name; where I am well known. I never lived with either of the men, but merely had a slight acquaintance with them. I gave them the money and duplicates.
One witness gave the prisoner, Richard Taylor, a good character, and one deposed the same for Timothy.
RICHARD TAYLOR - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 17.
TIMOTHY TAYLOR - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 21.
Of stealing in the dwelling-house, but not of burglariously breaking and entering.
CLIFFORD - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18240916-263]
Before Mr. Justice Littledale.
1503. MARY PICKERING and SARAH SHANNON were indicted for arson.
JOHN JACKSON. I am a cut-glass manufacturer, and live in Silver-street, Clerkenwell. The house No. 3, Castle-street, Clerkenwell, is my property. The prisoner Pickering was the occupier at the time in question, she is the widow of John Pickering, who has been dead about three months; he lived there for three years, she continued to live there after his death; he was my tenant, and had no lease: he paid twenty guineas a-year; he was a workman of mine; there was nearly a quarter's rent due at the time of his decease, she paid part of it, and I took one of his tools: she continued my tenant: her quarter's rent is not due untill Michaelmas: the house is my freehold.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. The husband was formerly in your employ - A. Yes, several years, she herself was nearly ten years in my service, and married from me. I have not seen the house since this happened, for I could not get in: Shannon lived with her, and I believe as a servant: Pickering had come to me about disturbances being made about the house, and I know that the windows are broken. I requested her to leave, and expected that she was about to leave.
MARY STACEY. I am the wife of James Stacey, and live at No. 3, Castle-street, Clerkenwell, in the house opposite Mrs. Pickering's; her's is No. 3, also. On the 26th of August, about a quarter or twenty minutes after ten o'clock at night, I saw Shannon go out of doors with a small bundle in her hand, Pickering followed her with a quart or pint mug, which, I cannot tell. Pickering put down the mug on the stones, and locked the door, and tried it after her: she went a little way, returned and looked through a little crack in the shutter, Shannon stood still until she came to her again, she said something to her, and they both went away together: three or fourminutes after they were gone, I was at my window up stairs, and saw a light through the crack in the shutter, it appeared to me for about half a minute to be gone out, and then to increase a great deal: my son was coming down the street, I called to him to go over and look through the crack, I saw nothing more.
Cross-examined by MR. LAW. Q. Shannon was Pickering's servant - A. I do not know, I have seen her there. Persons have collected before the house and broken the windows; that might be about three weeks before the fire: I do not think it happened more than once: the officers were there several times, through disturbances between Pickering and her sister. I have seen the neighbours about; the windows up stairs were all broken.
HENRY STACY. I am the last witness's son. On the 26th of August, about a quarter past ten o'clock, I was coming from work; my mother was looking through the window, and in consequence of what she said I went over and peeped through a crack in the shutters of Pickering's house, and saw a blaze in the room. I crossed over to my own door, and looked through the shutter again, and could see the light in the room - I immediately crossed again to convince myself whether it was a fire or not; and seeing that it was, I knocked at the door - no one was there to answer; some young men were talking at the corner of the street. I gave the alarm of fire - they came and broke the shutters open, and broke the windows. I ran home, and got nine or ten pails of water and threw it over the fire. Deadman broke the door open with a crow-bar. I went into the passage with a pail of water; the watchman shewed me a light while I broke the door open, and entered the room. There was a little bag of paper shavings on fire in the cupboard on the first shelf, and an old pair of bellows and some rags in the cupboard were also on fire. One or two of the cupboard shelves were on fire: there was no fire any where else. There was no candle or lamp there - a bed laid on the floor close to the cupboard. I did not notice whether the cupboard was a fixture, or whether it was built with the house - it had no door to it.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had you been there before you went over - A. Directly. I threw the water in from the street. The cupboard was close to the fireplace, and had no door to it; all the windows in the upper part of the house were broken a fortnight before. I have seen one or two hundred people making a disturbance before the house. I cannot say how often it was - I will not swear that it was ten times. Perhaps I have seen it six or seven times.
HARRIET NASH. I am the wife of John Nash, and live at No. 3, opposite to Pickering's house. On the evening of the 26th of August, I saw Pickering standing at her door; Shannon came up to her in a few minutes with a pot in her hand - it wanted about ten minutes to ten o'clock; they both went in, and shut the street-door after them - waited five or ten minutes, and then opened the street-door, and stood in the passage, and said something to each other. They then shut the parlour door - one of them had a bundle and a pewter pot on the pavement at the street-door. Then both came out - Shannon took up the pot and bundle and went on a little way, while Pickering locked the door, and tried it, and said,
"It is quite safe." She went on to overtake Shannon - returned again, and looked through a crack in the shutters. Shannon returned, and took hold of her, and with a horrid expression, said,
"Are you going to stay here all night? - I myself have made all that right." They had not been gone ten minutes before, I saw a glimpse of light through the shutters as I stood at the door. Stacy's son went and broke the shutters open, and gave an alarm. I did not go over - it is a narrow street, they talked loud enough for me to hear them on the other side.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you and them particular friends - A. Not particularly good or ill friends; if they had looked over, they must have seen me. Mrs. Stacy was at her window up stairs; there were often mobs about the house. I have often seen the officers there, but it was not owing to any of the neighbours; there was a gas lamp against their passage, which enables me to see into the passage.
CHRISTOPHER HUNT. I live in Turnmill-street. On the night of the 26th of August, I heard a cry of murder, and went to Castle-street. I went into the house after it was broken open, and saw a fire in the cupboard; there was a bed and mattress against the cupboard, and some shavings in the cupboard. I broke the shelves down - I saw no fire when I was in the house, but had seen it when outside; the rags and shavings had burnt as they laid at the bottom of the cupboard - the shelves were burnt: I knocked them down with a shovel. I do not know whether the cupboard was a fixture or not. I went up stairs, and found a deal table placed against the first floor door, outside; there was nothing in the room; some furniture was on the second floor, but none in the lower room that I noticed.
Cross-examined. Q. What furniture was there - A. I saw a chest of drawer, a mattress and bedstead on the second floor, and there might be chairs and tables. The table was put against the door, to prevent any one from getting in, as the windows were broken - they had been broken two or three weeks before.
JAMES DEADMAN. I live in Turnmill-street. On the 26th of August, I was at my door; an alarm of fire was given. I broke the street-door open with a crow bar, and found the shelves of the cupboard burning - I have been at the house before. I think that the cupboard was originally built with the house, as it communicates with the wainscoat - all the shelves and the side of the cupboard was burnt. I pulled it down; the shelves were fixed to the wainscoat, either by nails or screws; there was cuttings of paper, old rags, and a pair of old bellows, part of which were burnt - the mattress laid on the floor. I had seen it three weeks before precisely in the same situation. I put my hand to the grate, the ashes were quite cold, and there was not the least appearance of fire having been there that night - the fire had gone no farther than the cupboard; there was a table in that room.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you - A. A rag merchant.
Q. Do you mean a dealer in marine stores - A. You may call me so:
"Dealer in marine stores" is written over my door; I call myself a rag merchant: I deal in iron and lead. I have seen persons collected round the house several times; when I went there a fortnightbefore, there was a mob. I think I told the Magistrate that the side of the cupboard was burnt. The paper cuttings were white slips: I cannot say whether they are what people put into grates in the summer.
SAMUEL SEDDON. I am a watchman. I heard an alarm of fire, and went to the house about twenty minutes after ten o'clock, and saw fire in the cupboard in one corner of the room, the whole cupboard appeared to be on fire; it was rather abated by water being thrown on it. I got a lantern, and saw a bed and mattress on the floor, with one laid against the cupboard. I took a small heap out of the cupboard on a large shovel; it happened to be some paper shavings, a pair of stays, and the wood of a pair of bellows, they appeared all to have been on fire, but put out by water. On the morning of the 26th, to the best of my recollection, about a quarter or ten minutes after five o'clock, I saw the prisoners in Turnmill-street; Pickering seeing me, came over and said
"Watchman, people may think I have been out all night, being out so early in the morning, but I have not; I have been sleeping at a house which I have lately taken, for there is a bad lock on the door, and I was afraid to trust it, and now I am come down to see how this house goes on, for I have 200 l. worth of property there." Between a week or a fortnight before that, I saw some furniture carried out of her house early in the morning, it was loaded on a cart or van, she helped to carry some things; Shannon was with her. I went off duty, returned in about twenty minutes, and the cart was gone.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it on the morning before the fire that you saw her in the street - A. To the best of my recollection it was, but she told me before the Magistrate that it was Sunday. I have seen a number of persons collected round her house. The paper shavings were like some I have seen in grates.
WILLIAM CLARK. I am a constable. On the night of the 26th of August, as soon as the fire went out, I went with Rice to No. 15, Slade's-place; Pickering had taken that house, she was going to bed there - she asked what I wanted her for, I said I would tell her as soon as she got to the watch-house, and when we got there she asked me to be as good as my word. I said she was charged with setting her house on fire; she seemed much alarmed, and asked for water, and before that could be obtained, she went into hysterics. I went to the house next day, and brought away the cupboard, shelves, some burnt paper and rags.
Cross-examined. Q. How far is Slade's-place from the house - A. Three or four minutes walk. I knew where to find her.
RUEBEN RICE. I was with Clark, and apprehended the prisoners - they had lived in Slade's-place for nearly a fortnight. I have the cupboard shelves, stays, bellows, a gown, and pieces of paper.
Cross-examined. Q. The gown is a stuff one - A. Yes - it is not a thing which easily burns. She had slept at Slade's-place for a fortnight before the fire, and had moved part of her furniture there. I saw Pickering in Castle-street between three and four o'clock in the afternoon of the fire. I have frequently seen persons collected before the house, and she has sent for me to disperse them - she was obliged to move in consequence of it.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18240916-264]
Middlesex Cases, Second Jury.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1503. JAMES HAYES was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of September, two carpets, value 8 l., and thirty yards of stair carpet, value 3 l., the goods of William Ginger, in a certain out-house, belonging to his dwelling-house.
DAVID DAVIS. I am servant to William Ginger, who lives in College-street Westminster, and is a bookseller. On the 4th of September, about ten minutes past seven o'clock, I found the back door open on coming to the house, and on going further I found the warehouse door forced open. Some carpeting which I had left there a few nights before was scattered about the place, and about 12 l. worth gone. The warehouse does not join the dwelling-house, but is enclosed in one fence with it. The carpet belonged to the house.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. What do you use the warehouse for - A. We keep books there; the carpets were placed there as the family were out of town; they were kept in the warehouse or outhouse - we deposited books and paper there.
BENJAMIN TIMBRELL. I am an officer. On the 4th of September, about half-past five o'clock. I met the prisoner in Peter-street, with a bag on his shoulder, and asked what was in it; he said nothing. I found it contained carpeting, and took him to the watch-house.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not say that he did not know what was in it - A. No; his answer was
"Nothing;" he said nothing about finding it.
ELIZA COOK. I am housemaid to Mr. Ginger. I missed these carpets out of the warehouse.
Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was going to bathe, and saw the sack in a dust cart; nobody being near, I laid hold of it, and was coming along when this man took me. I said I hardly knew what I had.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Of stealing to the value of 39 s. only.
Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18240916-265]
1504. JOHN ENGLISH was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. WILLIAM WATERHOUSE. I keep the Swan with two Necks, in Lad-lane. The prisoner superintended my business at Hounslow, where I keep seventy or one hundred horses; the prisoner generally bought the hay for them by my directions: it was his duty to buy hay when I directed: he has done so for twelve or fourteen years; he told me I should soon be in want of hay; I told him to look out for some, and he wrote me a note, which I produce.
This note was here read. It was dated the 2d of February, and stated that he had bought ten loads of the best meadow hay of Mr. Buckland at 4 l. 10 s. a load.
Q. Did you see him after this - A. I cannot say whether I wrote or sent to him - (looking at a bill of parcels) - I have paid Mr. Buckland the amount of this bill; I directed the prisoner to have this hay delivered at Hounslow - I cannot say whether it was verbally or by letter; my stables were formerly known as the Old King'sHead: I had no horses any where at Hounslow but there.
Cross-examined by MR. ANDREWS. Q. What other establishment for horses have you - A. One at Staines. Buckland lives at Rainsley; ten or eleven miles from Hounslow. I expected the hay to be delivered the beginning of last February. I cannot say whether I sent him a message that it was to be delivered at Hounslow, or whether I wrote him or told him verbally. It was his duty to buy provender when he knew I was in want, but he generally informed me what he wanted before he bought.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Under your directions he was to buy hay and straw for you - A. He was.
Mr. ANDREWS. Q. Did you know that he bought any for Dallimore - A. No; I accused him of buying straw for Dallimore, being informed that he had, and he said he had. I said I would suffer no servant of mine to buy hay, straw, or corn for anybody but myself, and if he did so again, I should dismiss him - this was a year and a half ago: his reply was, that he could not get paid for what he had bought, and should buy no more.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. After this hay had been bought of Buckland, did the prisoner see you, and say anything about it - A. He said it had been delivered. He bought hay occasionally for the horses at Staines. No hay of mine would be delivered at the Lion and Lamb stables. My eldest son at times goes down and gives orders, but very seldom, for he lives on the North-road, and had been extremely ill for three months about February.
VIRGO BUCKLAND. I sold ten loads of hay to the prisoner for Mr. Waterhouse, at my house at Ranislury. The bill produced is what I delivered for it. The prisoner ordered it of me for Mr. Waterhouse. He told me to send it to Hounslow, two loads at a time - the two first were single loads. It was all delivered from the beginning of February to the end of March. I sent most of it by my man Rowe. Jackson is the boy who goes with him. I desired the carter to deliver it at the prosecutor's stables at Hounslow.
THOMAS ROWE. I am carter to Mr. Buckland. In the early part of this year I was employed by him to deliver some hay; and one day, when I was going with some to Mr. Waterhouse's the Old King's Head, the prisoner met me by the gateway of the Lion and Lamb, and told me to leave one load there - he saw me do it, and pitched it into the loft himself at Mr. Dallimore's stables. I took the other load on to the Old King's Head. The prisoner was there, and saw me deliver it.
Cross-examined. Q. Dallimore lives at the Lion and Lamb - A. Yes; the prisoner counted the trusses.
MR. WATERHOUSE. The prisoner never accounted to me for any hay delivered at the Lion and Lamb. I had forbidden him to sell any for himself.
Cross-examined. Q. All your money transactions with him will appear by your books - A. He paid me for no hay. My cash-book contains no money transactions with him.
Q. Suppose your stock of hay to be suddenly exhausted, what was he to do - A. To look out for more, and inform me; but we never run our stock so close; if such a thing should happen, (but it never did) he would go to respectable farmers, and could always get hay on my account: I know of one instance in which he bought hay, and told me of it - I paid him for it; and have been told that he has some land on Hounslow-heath: I never authorized him to sell me hay.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did he ever sell hay for you - A. I never authorized him to sell any at all, and never understood that he had sold any of that bought of Mr. Buckland; nor did he ever state that he had sent Dallimore any in re-payment for any he had borrowed, in a case of emergency; it is not at all probable that we should be out of hay.
JOHN SMART. I was horsekeeper to Mr. Dallimore, at the Lion and Lamb public-house, Hounslow. I paid the prisoner a bill of 12 l. 2 s. for two loads of hay and one of straw; it was delivered at the Lion and Lamb; I know that some of it came from Mr. Buckland's - Rowe brought it.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. You do not know when it was delivered - A. One load, which came from Buckland, was in February - and the other in March. I paid English for it between Christmas and Lady-day, and paid him 7 l. 2 s. before that.
JOSEPH HOWELL. I am servant to Mr. Dallimore. I paid English 12 l. 2 s. in April, for hay on Mr. Dallimore's account.
Prisoner's Defence. At times when short of hay I bought of jobbers, and at times supplied it from my own stock, and have then sent the prosecutor's hay to Dallimore, to repay myself; but whatever hay I took in this way I took care to supply in another.
GEORGE DAY. I am horse-keeper at the King's Arms public-house, Holborn-bridge. I lived with Mr. Waterhouse at Hounslow the beginning of this year; I knew the prisoner's premises at Hounslow very well; he had a hay-rick there; three loads and a half of hay were delivered at Mr. Waterhouse's yard from the premises; most of it was mixed up with the corn; we were short of hay at that time.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Were you present when the hay was cut - A. I saw it cut out of the rick, and I brought it from his place about three weeks after Christmas.
JOHN WHITE. I have known the prisoner for sixteen years; I know that he used to buy hay of jobbers - I cannot swear to more than one instance, which was six or seven months ago. We had bought some of a jobber for 5 l. a load: there were four or five bad trusses among it - the man changed them, and moved from my stables, and English bought them of him. He has six or seven acres of land; some of it is grass land - I give him as good a character as possible.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you come to Mr. Waterhouse with any overture - A. Yes.
Q. Now, I ask you, do you, on your oath, believe him to be an honest man - A. I do; I made no overture by his desire - it was out of friendship to him.
JOHN SPRINGALL. I am a farmer, and live on Hammersmith-common. I have sold English hay nine or ten times; a load and a half, or two loads at a time, he paid me for it.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. When did you sell him the last hay - A. Not since 1822, I believe - very likely it might be a year ago or rather better.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18240916-266]
NEW COURT, (7th DAY.)
Middlesex Cases, Third Jury, Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1505. THOMAS DOWSE was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of August, at St. Andrew, Holborn, a coat, value 2 l. a waistcoat, value 5 s. a pair of trowsers, value 10 s. a handkerchief, value 4 s. and a shirt, value 4 s. the goods of Catharine Dowse, widow, in her dwelling-house.
CATHARINE DOWSE. I live at No. 23, Cross-street, Hatton Garden, and am a widow. The prisoner is my son. On Tuesday, the 16th of August, about seven o'clock in the morning, I missed a coat, waistcoat, and trowsers, and a black silk handkerchief, belonging to another son of mine; they were safe the evening before. My cellar window was under repair; if any one had got in there, they might have got to the parlor, where these things were. The prisoner was brought in, and searched, in my presence. We found duplicates of the clothes, and 35 s. in money on him. He said nothing about the clothes; I got them back, and they are here now, The coat and waistcoat were in one place, and the trowsers in another.
JOHN WHITE DENNING. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Great Surrey-street. These trowsers were pledged at my house on the 17th of August, in the name of John Good. I gave a duplicate, but do not know the person.
JOHN LIMBRICK. I am an officer of Hatton Garden. I produce the duplicates.
JOHN FLOWER. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Shoe-lane. I produce a coat and waistcoat, which were pledged by a lad in court, (not the prisoner) on the 17th of August.
EDWARD BILLETT. I pledged these articles at the last witness's shop. They were given to me by the prisoner.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
CATHERINE DOWSE, re-examined. I saw my son two days afterwards; he is employed in carrying milk for me.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 15.
[trialAccount t18240916-267]
1506. JAMES ARMSTRONG, was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of August, at St. George's, Hanover-square, two coach lamps, value 7 s.; two saddles, value 30 s.; three collars, value 30 s.; a pair of stirrup-irons, value 1 s.; a pair of pole-pieces, value 2 s.; a pair of collar braces, value 2 s.; a martingale, value 10 s.; a pair of pole braces, value 1 s.; a chaise pad, value 15 s.; a pair of traces value 10 s.; a pair of reins, value 5 s.; five bridles, value 3 l.; and a gig harness, value 50 s.; the property of David Peek, his master, in his dwelling house.
DAVID PECK. I am a coach-maker, and live in Mount-street, Grosvenor-square, in the parish of St. George's, Hanover-square. The prisoner had been in my service as labourer for two or three years. On the 26th of August I enquired of him for some saddles and bridles; he said they were in the lumber-loft, he had put them there out of the way; I told him to go and look for them. I went up myself half an hour after, and forgetting what I had sent him for, I asked him what he did there. He said the things were not there, and he supposed they were down stairs. I came down, went out, and when I returned, I found he had gone out. He did not return. I searched the premises and missed the articles stated in the indictment; he had had them in his care. The gig harness is worth more than 40 s.; but it might have been taken separately, and the pieces would have been under that value.
JONATHAN DOBSON. I got acquainted with the prisoner last May; he spoke to me about lamps, and he sent some by his little boy; I told him I would not buy of any children, and he came himself. I bought those articles of him, and hung them up in my shop, which is at No. 12, Francis-court, Wigmore-street.
WILLIAM BALLARD. I did not apprehend the prisoner, but gave directions to a watchman, who apprehended him on the 25th of August. I told him I had been looking after him for some time for stealing harness; he said he should wish to see his master. I went with him to his master's. I said,
"Here is your master, will you tell him what you have done with the harness;" he then told me where the things were, and what he had got for them. Most of the articles are here: some were sold, and could not be got back again.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 46.
Of stealing to the value of 39 s. only.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18240916-268]
1507. MARY ANN COLLISON was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of September, three shirt bodies, value 18 s. and a pair of stays, value 3 s. the goods of Charlotte Hawthorn, widow.
CHARLOTTE HAWTHORN. The prisoner slept at my house on the 8th of September, and had been there a week. I went out that day about two or three o'clock, and left her with my child; I returned in about a quarter of an hour; I searched my drawers, and missed three shirt bodies and a pair of stays: I had locked the drawer when I went out; I had seen them about a fortnight before. I saw the shirt bodies again at Marlborough Street.
CHARLES WILLIAMS. I am a pawnbroker, and live in St. Martin's Lane. These things were pawned at my shop by a woman on the 8th of September.
JOSEPH COLLINS. I am an officer. On the 8th of September the prisoner was brought to the watch-house. I asked her what she had done with the shirt-bodies and stays; she said the shirt-bodies were pledged in St. Martin's-lane, and the stays she knew nothing about.
GUILTY. Aged 12.
Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18240916-269]
1508. MARY BURN and ANN MALONY were indicted for stealing, on the 31st of July, two pairs of boots, value 8 s., the goods of James Clough.
WILLIAM MOSS. I am a tailor. On the 31st of July, I was in my shop in Great Crown-court, and saw the two prisoners facing my house, near Mr. Clough's shop. Burn went into the shop, and immediately returned, folding something up in her apron; I looked out and saw them running; I went over to the shop, and asked if they had lost any thing. I could not follow them, because I had no person in my own shop. I saw Malony distinctly, and am positive to her person.
WILLIAM SABINE. Mr. Clough had these goods in his shop, and I found them afterwards. I cannot identify them.
NOT GUILTY.
