[trialAccount t18240916-272]
1511. JAMES ASKEW was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of September, five rings, value 80 l.; two pairs of ear-rings, value, 25 l.; part of a comb, set with diamonds, value 50 l.; a diamond cross, value 25 l.; a gold chain, value 10 l.; a gold buckle, value 4 l.; an opera-glass, value 2 l.; three silver spoons, value 3 l.; three silver forks, value 3 l.; thirty-nine pairs of stockings, value 3 l.; twenty-nine shifts, value 7 l.; a shawl, value 30 l.; a veil, value 5 l.; a lace handkerchief, value 3 l.; a dress, value 1 l.; and nineteen towels, value 10 s., the property of Elizabeth Virginie Perrot: and seven promisory notes for payment of 1000 francs each, in French money, value 41 l. 13 s. 4 d. each; and five other promissory notes for payment of 500 francs, value 20 l. 16 s. 8 d., the property of Victor Bertrand.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the prosecution.
ELIZABETH VIRGINIA PERROT. I became acquainted him, the prisoner, in Paris a year ago next month; I was first milliner in his house, and kept his house. My salary was 2000 francs per year; I was to have board and lodging, and washing. I continued seven months and seven days at that salary; it was then increased to double; soon after that I saw a gentleman of the name of Victor Bertrand, he came on a visit by the invitation of Mr. Askew; I am quite certain Mr. Askew invited him. Those letters are Mr. Askew's writing (looking at them) I know his writing very well; indeed when Mr. Bertrand came, in the latter end of April, or beginning of May, 1824, he placed some money, and some French Billet Banques of the Royal Bank in my hands. I went to Cheltenham on the 4th of July; the prisoner and I were then on very good terms. I did not take all my property to Cheltenham; I left the bills in my chest of drawers in the bedroom; they were in a piece of whitey-brown paper, together with this letter, which is from a nobleman. I know a gentleman of the name of Hubert, but I never told him the signature of this letter before he saw it. I also left all the property stated in the indictment. (enumerating them.) Mr. Bertrand had put the promissory notes into my possession, to take care of. All the jewellery was given to me, but two rings, which I bought. I went to Cheltenham on the 4th of July, and received all these letters from Mr. Askew. I remained on good terms with him until about the beginning of August, when a disagreement began, of which jealousy was the cause. I received a gold watch from Mr. Askew, as a present, a fortnight before he had me arrested; he arrested me on Saturday, the 14th of August, for the money I had received for him at Cheltenham, which amounted to 55 l. he at that time owed me about 70 l. I gave the 55 l. and 10 l. to gentleman who arrested me. Mr. Askew came down to Cheltenham, and caused me to be arrested himself. He turned me out of the house on Saturday evening; he signed a paper before I went out of the house; this is the paper (looking at it.) It was signed at the desire of my attorney at Cheltenham. (read)
"I have received all the bonnets, goods, &c. of Madammoiselle Perrot, delivered to her by me. Dated the 14th of August. J Askew." At the time he signed this paper, he owed me 70 l., which he has never paid me. I returned to London on Monday, and made application for my property to Mr. Askew, with a witness - (Mr. Hutchings,) but I never got it. When I went to Cheltenham, I left my drawers locked; I left an inventory of some things, but not all: this is it - it was written by Mr. Bertrand before I left Paris. In consequence of this refusal to give me my property, I sent Mr. Hubert for my property; Mr. Askew said,
"I have no objection to deliver to you your clothes, which are in the drawers, but not the bedstead and chest of drawers;" he said my clothes, were a few stockings and a few shifts, which I should have, but the furniture was not mine. I went a second time with Mr. Hubert, about three weeks after. Mr. Askew then said he would not deliver anything without his attorney; he did not deliver anything then to me. About a week after that, part of my property was brought by Mr. Hubert - a pair of diamond ear-rings, some stockings, and a few dresses, but no shifts. I had no other jewellery; the earrings were in the inventory; they are worth about 5 or 6 l. I was not present at the opening of my drawers. Before Mr. Hubert went to Mr. Askew's, I described to him something that was in my drawers; I told him there was a letter from a nobleman; I did not mention the inventory, I did not know that it was there; I received these ear-rings on the Saturday, about six o'clock in the evening, and made the complaint on Monday. I went to the magistrate with Bertrand; the magistrate decided the cause at the second sitting; my attorney was there and counsel, and Mr. Askew had advice likewise. When Mr. Hubert brought this property, I did not tell him that was all that I had left; I was very much hurt; I have never got my property; I am sure I left the bank-bills wrapt up in a paper with this nobleman's letter; I gave Mr. Hubert an inventory of my property as well as I could make it out; part of it is written by myself, and part by Bertrand; this is it (looking at it.)
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Who is the writer of this letter - A It is a Nobleman, and I should be sorry his name should be stated; I will not tell his name; it is the first General in the army of the Emperor of Russia, Nicolas Switzer. He desired me to direct to him as His Excellency. I never heard him called by any other title but Excellence or General. I did not bring all these things from Paris: Bertrand brought some things. The clothes were not all included in the inventory which Bertrand made out in Paris; there are some things in Paris still. Bertrand brought me 29 shifts from Paris, a black silk dress, and some jewellery; the comb, the cross, and the small chain, the opera glass, the three spoons and three forks, another gold chain which I now have, and some other things which I cannot recollect: that is not all the linen and clothes he brought over; he brought over a lace veil. I never made an inventory of the things he brought over. I lived at No. 4, Rue de Grenelle, at Paris, when the prisoner came and engaged me. This letter is Mr. Bertrand's hand-writing (looking at it;), the direction is his writing. I think it was in my drawer, but I have received a great many letters from Bertrand, and cannot be positive, as I have not seen the inside; it is the writing of Bertrand, but addressed to Harriet Duland; she was brought by Mr. Askew as a witness. That letter has never been in my drawer. This is also Bertrand's writing;it is a declaration to Mr. Abraham, an attorney, who lives in the City, near Temple Bar. He told me he had written it; I was close by him when he wrote it, but it was not by my direction; he did not read it to me. I saw Mr. Abraham several times with him; I don't recollect that I ever read it or saw it till now; I swear that; I don't recollect hearing it read; I don't know when it was written; it was written since my return from Cheltenham: I think it was before I employed Mr. Hubert. I had been con sulting Mr. Abraham about Mr. Askew's not delivering to me my chest of drawers and all the furniture of my bed-room. I went to Mr. Abraham before I employed Mr. Hubert. I don't know whether this paper was written to Mr. Abraham as instructions what he was to do; there is no part of my writing on the outside of that paper. I applied to Mr. Abraham perhaps about a fortnight before I went to Mr. Hubert, to whom I was recommended by the French Consul. I never mentioned to any one about the 9500 francs as being too much for me to lose: I mentioned it to Mr. Hubert when I begged him to apply for it. I never mentioned to Mr. Abraham or his clerk the particular things that were in the drawers. Mr. Abraham gave me an order to fetch my things from Mr. Askew, but not the jewellery: he was not employed for the jewellery, but for my agreement and the furniture of my bed-room. I may have told him about the jewellery, but as I expected to get the furniture, I thought I should get that. I don't recollect that I told him about it. I told him of a bill which I had paid Mr. Askew for my furniture. I don't recollect whether Mr. Abraham asked me for particulars, that he might make a demand; I can't swear it. This is the list I made out for Mr. Hubert to make the demand; part is my writing and part Bertrand's Mr. Bertrand lived in the Rue de Grenelle, No. 39; he is no relation of mine; he was a grocer; I have called him my brother by the order of Mr. Askew. Mr. Askew told me so in the carriage from Paris to London. Bertrand was not coming to England then, but he was invited to England by Askew. Mr. Askew gave me a particular reason for calling Bertrand my brother; I used to write to him very often: Mr. Askew desired me to call Bertrand my brother, for the reputation of his house, after Bertrand arrived in London, but not before; I said so at the Police Office; but the first time he told me to call him my brother was in the carriage. He then gave me no reason at all for it. I had never called Bertrand my brother before I came to London, but always spoke of him as such at Mr. Askew's. Mr. Askew told me in Bertrand's shop,
"My desire is to make you happy when you come to London, and Bertrand will be there by and by." Bertrand gave me the Billet de Banque in the beginning of May; he brought them with his own things in his trunk, which was locked: it was kept in my bed-room, and I always had the keys; there were two locks, one was a padlock. Bertrand had been in London but a few days when he gave me the Billet de Banque; I was in my bed-room when he gave them to me out of his pocket-book to keep for him. Mrs. Hudson saw them one morning, and Bertrand came in and scolded me for it; that was about a month before I went to Cheltenham. Bertrand had some French and English gold beside. He never borrowed any money from me or any body to my knowledge. He had some bills which were not Billet de Banque, and I think he had some other Billet de Banque; he gave me more, and he used to go to the drawer, and take out what he wanted. I will not swear that I have seen him with any other Billet de Banque; I do not recollect when Bertrand came to England. He used to sleep in a warehouse at Mr. Askew's house; he removed from there about three weeks before I went to Cheltenham; he went to live in Berwick-street, at Mrs. Broadfoot's; Mrs. Askew took the lodging for him. I have sent him sometimes little different things, when he used to give me the money; Mr. Askew gave me permission to do so; he had a quarrel with Bertrand before I went to Cheltenham, and Mr. Askew forbade him the house, but gave me permission to send him some victuals; after I was at Cheltenham, Askew told me that he continued to send him things, and wrote so to me. He continued to lodge at Mrs. Broadfoot's, and I lodged there on my return from Cheltenham. Mrs. Broadfoot had never turned him away up to the time I left London for Cheltenham. Although Bertrand was not in the house, I continued to supply him with provisions, but not at Askew's expence: Bertrand paid very dear for it; the Billet de Banque were left in my possession because he went into furnished lodgings, and they are not always very safe. I told Bertrand on the Saturday evening, that I should bring them to him on Sunday morning before I went to Cheltenham, but did not, because we went too early - myself and two young ladies. I went on Sunday morning; I knew that I was going on the Thursday; Mr. Askew had before desired me to go, but I always refused, and even on Saturday night, when my box was packed up, I had not agreed to go. On the Thursday, or the day before, Mr. Askew returned from Cheltenham, and said he had taken a house, and he hoped I would go to oblige him, but I had not agreed to go; I was very cross with him on Saturday night; I had been to Bertrand, and promised to give him his billet de banque on Sunday morning; I saw him between ten and eleven o'clock at night; he lives but a little way from Mr. Askew, who lives at No. 85, Oxford-street, nearly opposite the Pantheon. Mr. Askew went to Bertrand for him to come again, and persuade me to go to Cheltenham; he had before talked of taking a house in the country, and wanted Bertrand to go and keep it for him; I did not, upon my oath, see Bertrand on the Sunday morning, before I set off; I went at six o'clock, and it was a wet morning, which was the only reason I did not give him the Billet de Banque; I had to dress myself and to breakfast, I never left any direction with Bertrand to go and take any of these things. I never took any account or number of these bills - it is not the custom in France. There were some of them like this one, but I cannot tell whether they are all alike; I did not look at them particularly. I did not take many white stockings to Cheltenham; I took five or six pair, and was obliged to buy some there. Mr. Askew told me not to take much with me, because unless it pleased me, I need not stay there. I did not take even a white dress; I was obliged to buy some. I did not write to Mr. Askew for any of my things; I was afraid to give up my key, because I had some money and some jewellery there. Some peoplehave seen my jewellery; I have worn the ring bearing the portrait of the Emperor of Russia, but I do not know whether any body saw it. I had another ring of somebody else. I never wore any diamonds since Mr. Duland was here; I would not dress myself like a lady to be in the showroom; Miss Hudson saw some of my jewellery in my drawer; I may have taken her to show it her, but I do not recollect. I had a coral neck-lace, but I never said or swore that it was one of the things that was taken from me. I have said, that Bertrand had some other Billet de Banque and some gold. I live now in Berwick-street, at Mrs. Broadfoot's house. I never told Mrs. Broadfoot that I was married to Bertrand; but she is a woman to swear that which is untrue, because she is paid for it: I know it, a man told me so; I do not know his name, but it was the man that arrested us for the lodging; it was a distress, and my attorney has been kind enough to pay, because Mr. Askew has taken my all. The day Mr. Askew was taken to the Office, Mrs. Broadfoot's young boy was there, and said all manner of things. The man said that he saw another man who came to Mrs. Broadfoot's house, and spoke to Mrs. Broadfoot, and she said she would swear anything against me. Mrs. Broadfoot's son said he got 5 s. and his mother 5 s. and their victuals, and a coach to fetch them, and so they would swear any thing, and that is the truth. Mrs. Broadfoot never asked for her rent until Mr. Askew was arrested; that was in the presence of Bertrand; it is since Mr. Askew went to Newgate. I have not told her upon any occasion that I had 15 l. in my drawer at Askews, and with that I would pay her; but when we came from Cheltenham, Mr. Bertrand said, we have no money, but if she would be kind enough to let us into her house, we would pay her in November, or when we got our furniture and things from Mr. Askew's. When Mr. Askew had refused to deliver my things, I have said
"That is 15 l. out of my pocket;" because Mr. Askew had not delivered to me my furniture, which was worth 15 l.; I paid for it, and have got my receipt. I have said to Mrs. Broadfoot
"I will pay you with 65 l. which is in the hands of a man at Cheltenham." I had a letter from Mr. Askew, communicating the contents of this French letter, and I was afraid of it; it was not a long time before he was arrested; it was before I sent Mr. Hubert to demand my goods, but after I applied to Mr. Abrahams. The night that I came from the Office, I saw Mrs. Broadfoot at her house; it was on the 15th of September Mr. Askew was committed; I saw her every night. I never said,
"I will have my revenge; I will hang him," though he has robbed me, it is not my wish to hang him. I never said to her,
"Askew has gone to Newgate; I will have my revenge! I will have his life!" Mrs. Broadfoot did not say,
"Stop, stop, not so fast! he is not tried yet." I know Mr. Unit, but I never told him that Bertrand had brought over 2,000 francs only, and had changed them at a shop in Regent-street; but I told Unit, to prove that Bertrand had brought some money with him, that I myself had changed 200 francs for him in St. James's-street. Bertrand never told him that he had brought over 2,000 francs only; but he told him, in my presence, about the 200 francs. I did not tell Unit that the produce of these 200 or 2,000 francs was locked up in my drawer at Mr. Askew's, nor did Bertrand; but I said I had exchanged 200 francs about three weeks ago. Mr. Askew said, his reason for forbidding Bertrand his house was, because Bertrand was my friend, and it was on account of jealousy. I had none of the jewellery at Cheltenham.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You said to Mr. Adolphus that you had lost 15 l. through not having the furniture; is this the receipt for it? - (Looking at it) Yes. I told Mrs. Broadfoot, after I had a letter from Mr. Askew, that I would pay her in November, with the 65 l. in the Sheriff's hands at Cheltenham. The letter from Mr. Askew is not signed, but his own servant brought it; here is a translation of the French letter. I went to Cheltenham in July; Mr. Askew and I had quarrelled. Bertrand was then receiving things from Mr. Askew; he was still trying, notwithstanding the quarrel, to get Bertrand a situation. In July, when I went to Cheltenham, I had but one key to my drawers, and I took it with me. I am sure I left the bills and jewellery in the drawers. When I was with Bertrand on Saturday night I did not know I was to go so early as six; Askew never told me the hour. Mr. Abraham never got me any thing, and then the French Consul recommended me to Mr. Hubert, and I employed him. Some of the jewellery was not in the inventory in the drawer; it was only of the things I brought with me to London. I heard the Russian gentleman called His Excellence, and that is why I called him a Nobleman. When I was travelling in the coach with Askew, he was very kind and attentive to me, and I objected to it, because, I said,
"You are married, and I am engaged to Mr. Bertrand." I used to write to Bertrand very often, and Mr. Askew said,
"You must call him your brother." I said,
"He is not;" and Mr. Askew said,
"Never mind, you must call him so." Miss Dulan came with Mr. Askew; I did not desire it. I don't know whether she was examined on my part or not.
A. WATTS. I drew this agreement (looking at it), by the mutual instructions of the prisoner and Miss Perrot; Bertrand was there but gave no instructions. It was left in the possession of Miss Perrot; there was a copy of it, which Mr. Askew had. I don't know whether Miss Perrot had it then; for while it was in our custody, a message came from Mr. Askew, not to deliver it. That was about two days after we had it. It was five days or a week before Miss Perrot got it: she had called twice in that time. I mean positively to swear that there was a copy of it, and Mr. Askew had one part of it at the time of the execution, and I think he put it in his pocket. I am not certain whether I took it away with me, or it was given to us afterwards by some other person, one of the parties. I do not know how many times Miss Perrot and Bertrand called for it before they got it. I will swear they did not use threats to get it; there were two parties to it. Mr. Askew had one, and the other party one; it has not been altered in any way since I wrote it.
VICTOR BERTRAND. I came to England in the end of April this year. I know Miss Perrot. Three or four days after I came to England, I put into her hands seven billets de banque, of 1000 francs each, and five of 500 francs each, to take care of. I have never got them back.
Cross-examined by MR. LAW. Are these your handwriting (handing some letters.) - A. I think they are; but I do not very well know my own hand-writing. I have knownthe last witness four years. I do not know how many times she has seen me write; I have written many letters to her. I came from France in the month of April. I am sure I was not here in March. I did not make any memorandum of the notes I brought with me; it is not the custom in France; they were of the Bank of France; I do not know what Bank they were. The bills for 1000 francs were the same as the 500 franc bills in all respects except the amount. The 1000 franc billets de banque were like this one. I was a grocer at Paris. When I came here I left the business; I do not now carry it on. The bills I left with Miss Perrot were seven for 1000 francs, and five for 500 francs; I do not know whether I left more with her. I made an inventory for her when I was in Paris. I do not recollect whether I told her the amount of the bills; I gave them to her all together to take care of, in her bedroom. I have seen her open the drawers in my presence. I recollect they were put into a drawer; but I cannot tell what drawer. There was a box in her bed-room. I have no doubt that this is a translation of one of Miss Perrot's letters, made by herself - (looking at it) - I was at Mr. Askew's house at the time I gave the bills to Miss Perrot, and continued there about a month. I gave her the bills to take care of for fear of losing them at my furnished lodgings. I am not sure whether my box was in that room then. I did not put the bills into the box, because it is not comfortable to put them in with clothes. I received the bills in my shop. I cannot say that I took them all in the shop, but when I got 500 francs I have exchanged them in the Rue de Banc. I cannot tell the name of any one person from whom I received any one of them. I have been to an agent of exchange several times, but I do not know his name. I received one 1000 francs bill from a servant of the director of the prisons, Mons. Ronne; he is not a changer of money. I had more money in England. I had one more 1000 francs bill; I changed that near the Haymarket three or four weeks after I came here. I have not changed any other French paper money. I have not frequently received money from Perrot: I have never received any of her. I never received any communication respecting a situation as teacher, through a person named Pascan; I do not know the name. I never received any communication respecting my becoming teacher of French in a school, or heard any thing of it; and never applied for 30 l. to enable me to take it. When I became acquainted with Miss Perrot she was a very respectable person. I never went to her drawers to take money. It is possible I might have her keys, but I do not remember it. I had some French gold in the drawer. Sometimes I went to her drawer - very seldom - two or three times. Mr. Askew told me I should pass as her brother: he did object to my intimacy with her; that was always the subject of our quarrel. I have lodged in the same house with her, at Mrs. Broadfoot's. I did not say to Mrs. Broadfoot yesterday, that if she proved any thing against Miss Perrot or me, I would say she had asked me to sleep with her. I have said to her in her house,
"Your conduct is abominable; you send your son to the Magistrate and destroy my character, and you know what I know, and what I can say." Mr. Askew took the lodging for me in that house; I had no quarrel with Mrs. Broadfoot till I found she was to be a witness against me. I was once going up stairs at Mr. Askew's, and Miss Perrot was showing my bills to Miss Hudson, and I said,
"Why do you show them?" Miss Hudson was then in the room or on the same floor: Miss Hudson has told me she has seen them. I never said at Cheltenham, in Miss Hudson's presence,
"Mr. Askew is a very bad man, and I will bring him as low as the ground." I never had any thing good to say of him. I have conversed with Mrs. Broadfoot, but won't undertake to state what I have said of Mr. Askew; I have always looked at him as the dirt. I never said I was married to Perrot, or that we were man and wife, nor has she in my hearing.
Mr. PHILLIPS. Q. When you intended to come to England, did you keep a sum of money for that purpose - A. Yes. I brought near 11,000 francs. I gave some gold to Miss Perrot; I have been to her drawer for it. The Bank in Paris is a public Bank; I did not know the name of any person there. The gentleman who's servant brought me one of the bills, is a director of the prisons. I had never quarrelled with Broadfoot, till after Askew was complained of; on our arrival from Cheltenham she treated us as her children.
GEORGE HUBERT. I am an attorney. I was engaged by Miss Perrot about the 1st of September. I went by her direction to Mr. Askew's, and demanded the property, of which she had furnished an inventory; this is the inventory; I believe I did not produce it the first time I went, but I requested a settlement of accounts between them; I claimed upwards of 70 l. for wages, and also the property she stated to be in his possession. He said what property she had was locked up in her chest of drawers; he said he had great complaints to make against her - she was a bad woman. I asked him how he could convince me of that; he said he had documents in his possession which would prove it; I requested him to show me them; he said
"If I knew you better I would." I said
"One document would be more convincing than anything he could say." Before I went to him, Madamoiselle Perrot had described a certain document which she said was in a chest of drawers, and when I asked Mr. Askew to show me the document on which he relied, he went up stairs, and brought down a letter; I read it two or three times. He said, perhaps, I did not understand the French language; he had a translation of it. I did understand it, and I said it was extremely extraordinary that he should be in possession of a confidential letter, and asked him how he had obtained it; he said it was of no consequence how he had obtained it; but he had got it. Madamoiselle Perrot had described a letter from a foreign nobleman, as being in her drawer, and this is the letter; the translation corresponds with the letter; the signature had not been told me; Madamoiselle Perrot said it was in French; she told me the name of the person who wrote it, but there is no name on the letter, nor does it appear to have come from a foreign nobleman. Nothing further passed about it, and soon after I went away; this was on Saturday, the 4th of September, and the next time I saw him was on Tuesday, the 7th of September; I had in the mean time seen Miss Perrot, and she had given me a document, which I have now in myhand. I see by an indorsement on the back of this paper, that it was the first time I saw him that I showed it him, and demanded the property. On the second interview, I waited on him with Miss Perrott, and requested him to pay her the wages which she claimed; and also, to deliver the property in her chest of drawers; and I said I thought, as the parties had complained against each other, the best way would be for him to give up the property she claimed, and then I should advise her to have the agreement given up, and it should be cancelled. He said she had already broken the agreement. I said I would not discuss that subject then. He said he would do nothing without consulting his solicitor; I then left him, requesting him to name an early day to settle the business; he named the next day, at four o'clock, but I did not go, in consequence of some intimation from Madamoiselle Perrot, but I went on the following Saturday alone. I had met him in the morning in the City, and it was agreed, that I should wait upon him at four o'clock. I requested that she should go with me to identify her property; he strongly objected to that; I urged it several times without effect; but he said, if she would write an order for him to deliver the property to me, and give me the key, it should be delivered to me. I am quite sure he mentioned the delivery of the key. I went at four o'clock, and took an order which I had written, and Miss Perrot had signed. I had shown him the inventory on the first occasion. At the third interview I was shown into the room, but just as I had opened the drawer, he went away. I believe there were two ladies and a Gentleman in the room. I did not find the goods corresponding with the inventory, but I found some of them, and I made an inventory of what I did find. Here is the inventory I made. I found a pair of diamond ear-rings, which are these, but no other articles of jewellery. I found an inventory in the drawers, in French: here it is. I did not search the drawers for any other articles than what were in the inventory she had given me. I could find no Bank Bills or the confidential letter she described.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. When did you see Mr. Askew first on this business - A. On the 4th of September; I had two interviews with him before I saw Etherington and Gregory. I did not leave the inventory or a copy of it with him, nor a demand in writing, but read the articles from the inventory. I said, I came from Miss Perrot, and the Consul had written me a letter on the subject. I did not say, I came from the French Consul. He afterwards showed me the letter; it was the third interview, I had the key, and went into room, but at the the first interview, he produced the letter. When I demanded the goods, at the second time, I proposed that they should give up the agreements, and they should be cancelled. If Miss Perrot had received her property and wages, we should have had no ground of complaint. I read the letter twice over: it was such a one as, perhaps, a Lady would not like to have made public; I should think it was of a nature to irritate the mind of Miss Perrot; she has been very much vexed at its being in Mr. Askew's possession; she has not expressed to me that it has been shown to other persons. I went before a Justice, about the beginning of September. I cannot recollect the date when I was requested to attend at Marlborough-street, by Miss Perrot. I saw Mr. Conant on the subject; it was before the goods were delivered up to me, I believe, but I cannot swear it.
NOT GUILTY.
Middlesex Cases, fourth Jury.

Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1512. GEORGE SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of August, at St. James, Westminster, a watch, value 37 s.; a seal, value 15 s.; a watch-key, value 10 s.; and a watch-chain, value 1 s. the goods of John Clarke, in his dwelling house.
HANNAH CLARKE. My husband's name is John Clarke; we live at No. 5, Silver-street, Clerkenwell Green; we have two shops next to each other. On the 6th of August my husband's watch was in the back parlour of one of the shops. I had left that shop a few minutes to go to the other, and on returning I saw the prisoner coming out of the back parlour; he came into the shop and asked for something, but what he I said don't know. I enquired what he wanted there; he said,
"Nothing, mistress; I have not touched any thing." I caught hold of him, he struggled, and I began to scream out. He took this watch from his pocket or his bosom, I cannot say which, and laid it in the window.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
REUBEN RICE. I am an officer. I went on the 6th of August and took the prisoner; the last witness had detained him. He said he hoped we would excuse him; he had nothing about him.
The Prisoner put in a written defence, stating that he had no idea of the watch being taken by any one; there were other persons in the shop at the time, and it must have been deposited by some one else in the place where it was found; he had nothing about him.
HANNAH CLARKE re-examined. Q. Are you quite sure the prisoner took the watch from some part his person - A. Yes.
One witness gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 16.
[trialAccount t18240916-274]
1531. THOMAS JAMES was indicted for the wilful murder of Thomas Smith .
EDMUND SMITH. I am the brother of Thomas Smith, the deceased. I was present on the 12th of July, in a field near Copenhagen-house; the prisoner was there: he was what they call bottle holder. My brother fought with one Harry Bostock, for two hours and seventeen minutes: I was in the field, but not with my brother. On the Thursday previous I went into my father's house, and then heard that my brother was to fight on the Monday. I never saw Bostock before the fight, nor since; my brother had lived fellow servant with him, and when he left they were always in opposition; I do not know that my brother challenged him to fight: but when I got to the field I went to my brother, and tried to persuade him not to fight; it was then about a quarter past eight o'clock; he said he was not going to fight, for the other party had not come, and they had forfeited the money - the others then came; my brother said they had forfeited the money; they replied that he was afraid to lose, and then they fought.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. I did not hear the prisoner endeavouring to persuade them to leave off. I expected it would be over every minute, and therefore did not go for an officer. My brother said before he fought that if he did not fight he should never be able to stand in Holborn to get a passenger.
JOHN MOULTON. I was present at the fight; I saw the prisoner act as bottle holder; he did not go for that purpose, but was asked, because there was a person to come, who did not come.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18240916-275]
1514. GEORGE WETENHALL was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of August, a gelding, price 70 l., the goods of Horace Price.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
RICHARD POWTS. I am a veterinary-surgeon, and live in Grosvenor-mews. I saw the prisoner at Wreckin-tavern, in Covent-garden, and had some business with him - he deals in horses, and lives at Kensington. He said he wanted a horse for a gentleman at Streatham of the name of Sewell; I said I had a very clever horse in my stable, if he would give the money; he said that would not be an object if he liked him. I said I could not well send it, but a porter came from him next morning, and I gave him a note to go and get the horse. I was to meet him in the afternoon, at his stables, to settle about it - I met him, and he said he it was a likely horse; he thought it would suit Mr. Sewell, who he expected every moment to try him; I said I would call about one or two o'clock, which I did, and found the prisoner, but the horse was gone. He said the gentleman had rode him, and liked him - if I would meet him at four o'clock he would settle with me. I met him at that time; he said,
"I can't do it now, I have to go into the City," but if I would meet him at twelve o'clock next day he would settle it; I went, and he again said he was going into the City, I must call at two. I went - he was not ready then, but said he was going to his brother at the Stock Exchange, to receive the money, and if I would meet him at four o'clock he would settle it: he then put me off till six o'clock - I went at six, but he was not ready - I told him I suspected he was not doing right. He then wanted me to wait till Monday, which I refused, and he put it off till the next day, but I did not get it then. I then went to Streatham, but could find no such person as Mr. Sewell.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you give the prisoner the horse to sell for you - A. Yes, to Mr. Sewell. It belonged to Mr. Horace Price, who is here. I should not have delivered it to him if he had not named Mr. Sewell, as it was not my own, or I might have done so.
COURT. Q. Was Sewell's name mentioned to you after you had agreed to part with the horse to him - A. No, Sir, before.
HORACE PRICE. I never empowered the prisoner to demand or take away the horse from Powis.
Cross-examined. Q. When you placed your horse in Powis's hands your object was to get money for him, and if you got it you did not care from what hand it came - A. No.
RICHARD POWIS re-examined. Q. Did you not state to the prisoner that you wished to do business with him, and to begin in a small way - A. No, I swear that; it was the first time I talked with him. I have met him there several times. I had some dealings and conversation with him about two horses of mine: he said he had had horses of Pennington, but I did not say I should be glad to deal with him on the same terms, nor that that was a month's credit.
Witnesses for the Defence.
RICHARD WETENHALL. I have seen Mr. Powis and the prisoner together, in conversation, about Mr. Powis's mode of dealing with Mr. Pennington - it was before the prisoner had Mr. Price's horse. Mr. Powis said that if the prisoner could let him have the money in a month or six weeks he should be perfectly satisfied, and it would be better to begin with one or two horses in a small way; this was the first week in August. I have a distinct and perfect memory about it. I know of no dealings that they had had before.
CHARLES GIBLING. I am servant to the prisoner. In August last I was present when he talked with Mr. Powis about two chestnut horses. I went with a note to Mr. Powis and fetched them without money: we had them a week or ten days before they were parted with. Mr. Powis came almost every day to see my master. I was sent for to the Wreckin-tavern, where Powis and my master were at dinner. I got an order from them when they were at the Wreckin, and the next morning I got the horse. Mr. Powis came to our stables, I think the next day; he asked how the horse was; I said it looked very well: he said it was seventy-five guineas; the horse was then at the stable, and they talked then about it. There was no other bargain made; it was to be sold by my master.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. A. When you went to the Wreckin, your master and Mr Powis were at dinner - A. Yes; I do not know what was said before - I do not know Mr. Sewell of Streatham; I went soon after six o'clock next morning, and delivered the note to George, his man; he gave me the horse; it was then put into our stables, and stood there a good while; it never was sold.
RICHARD WETENALL re-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Are you a relation of the prisoner - A. Yes - his cousin; I live at No. 9, Regent-street, Charing-place; I lived before that at Park-street, Kennington-cross: I was at Mr. Wetenhall's house at the time of this conversation: on the 1st of September there was some talk about a horse being sold for seventy-five guineas - the subject arose about three horses; Mr. Powis said he wanted the money for his horse - the prisoner said he was going out of town, but if he could wait a month he would give him the money; Mr. Powis said he should be quite satisfied. On the 2nd of September my cousin went down to Kingston: he continued there till the Monday, when he and his wife returned to town; he has been in town ever since.
RICHARD WETENHALL, SEN. I am the prisoner's uncle. On the 1st of September I was at his house - I went after dinner: Mr. Powis said to the prisoner,
"I want my money for the three horses which you have bought of me - I request you will pay me." I know nothing about the horse of Mr. Price; the prisoner said,
"I cannot pay you now - I will in a month, or in the course of this month;" Mr. Powis said
"That will do very well, if you will he punctual:" Powis then said to me.
"You are a relation of the prisoner?" I said Yes: he then said,
" George Wetenhall is a good fellow as ever lived, and you will have no objection to drink his health in a bumper:" the name of Sewell, or Streatham, was never mentioned - I never had heard the name, upon my oath.
NOT GUILTY.

[trialAccount t18240916-279]
Before Mr. Recorder.
1517. JOSEPH HARWOOD was indicted for feloniously assaulting John Sheehan, in an open place, near the King's highway, on the 7th of July, at Isleworth, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, a coat, value 50 s.; a waistcoat, value 6 s.; a handkerchief, value 2 s.; an umbrella, value, 2 s.; a shirt, value 6 s.; a pair of shoes, value 2 s., and twelve shillings, his property.
JOHN SHEEHAN. I am a journeyman tailor, and live at Hampton-court. On the 7th of July I went to see the review on Hounslow-heath, and staid there till late in the evening. I was in a booth at the time this happened. I had taken a little drink, but swear that I was quite sober. I went into the booth between nine and ten o'clock with a young man who works with me; he is not here - and in a quarter of an hour, as I was coming out of the booth, (it was light,) I put my hand into my pocket, and took out half a crown to pay for some beer; and when I had paid the reckoning, some person struck me behind on the head - it was a severe blow; I could not see who it was - I was then dragged by two persons out of the booth - one of them laid hold of my arm. I had about 12 s. or more in my pocket. About twelve people then came and formed a circle round me, and I was shoved from one to the other for two or three minutes, and then the prisoner came up on my left side, and put his hand into my waistcoat pocket, and then into my trowsers pocket. I had two half-crowns in my waistcoat pocket; I put my hand to it after he had taken his out, and found the money was gone. I could not make the least resistance; there were so many people about me - I was afraid to resist. After he took his hand out of my trowsers pocket, I received a severe blow in the face, which knocked one of my teeth out. I was knocked down on the ground, and received several kicks. I got up, and then my coat was taken off my back. I then made towards the booth to save my life; they got between me and the booth, and prevented my getting in, and hawled me three or four yards from the booth. I was again knocked down on the ground - and one of them came and jumped on my legs, sprained it (I was under the doctor's hand with it) my waistcoat and shirt were then taken off my back, and the handkerchief off my neck, and the shoes off my feet - an umbrella was taken from me at the time; they took my coat - I was left naked all but my trowsers and stockings - my money was all taken.
Q. The prisoner you say is one of the party who illused you, and took your money? look at him, and say if you can safely swear that he was one of them - A. I can, my Lord; he kept staring me in the face before this ill usage began. I had observed him staring at me, and can safely swear that he is the man - he was a stranger before. Nobody came to my assistance; and after my clothes were gone, I was naked. I was hopping about towards the barracks, calling
"Murder;" they all left me when they had stripped me. I called
"Murder" while they attacked me also; I made it known next morning, but did not go before a Magistrate till evening, in consequence of my leg being so bad. I went before Mr. Trimmer at Histon, and was obliged to apply to the overseers of the parish, being so ill, and went into the workhouse to be cured; I was fetched from the workhouse about a week afterwards, and saw the prisoner in custody, and was certain of his person, and stated so to the Magistrate. I am quite sure of it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is not your name O'Sheehan - A. No. I had drank part of three pints of beer, and had some bread and cheese between me and another man that day, and I had part of a pot of beer with another man at six o'clock that evening, and about seven I had about three pints in a booth, and had nothing more all day. I went to the review at twelve o'clock, but did not go to drink till half-past four, when it was over. I drank nothing else. None of my companions were drunk while I was with them. I was a little fresh, but nothing to affect me. This happened about half-past nine o'clock, it was light. I did not see the prisoner till I came out of the booth; he was not one of the three who first came up. They did not shove me about till he came up; they surrounded me, and then he came up to me. I did not observe him before. I was frightened - for I had cause so to be. There were at least twelve people around me: I never saw either of them before.
JURY Q. Was it a rainy day - A. It was a rainy morning before the review commenced; it cleared up between three and four o'clock, and did not rain again.
EDWARD COLLINS. I am a shoemaker, and was at the review, acting as servant to Mr. Cook, who keeps a booth. This happened thirty or forty yards from our booth. I heard a cry of murder between nine and ten o'clock and came out of the booth, and saw a party of people a little way from our booth; I went up, and saw them taking hold of Sheehan, and were dragging him on the ground. Theyforced an umbrella out of his hand. He got up, and I saw him knocked down. There were twelve or thirteen people surrounding him, and all going on ill using him. They then got his coat off his back - he attempted to keep it under his arm. He made his escape into a gingerbread stall with the coat under his arm; they rushed after him, and took away his coat. I saw him bleeding at the mouth, he walked very lame, and appeared to be hurt in the leg. They all came again, and took him from the stall round to the back part of a publican's booth; I could not see what they did there, but in five minutes I saw him without shirt, waistcoat, and handkerchief; he had nothing but his trowsers and shirt, and appeared to have been much hurt. I could distinguish several of the persons. I saw the prisoner there; I am quite certain that he is one of them. I was afraid to interfere to assist him. It was light - I could very easily distinguish a person's features. The rain had cleared up about three o'clock, and it was a fine evening. I attended before the Justice about a week after, and saw him in custody, and am certain he was one of the men. I swear positively to him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you go up into the crowd while this was going on - A. I was not in the crowd; I was looking at it; I heard murder cried, and went to see what it was. Every time they took anything they ran from him.
Q. Why not run to some house and give an alarm - A. If I had run, very likely I should be attacked by some of them; I did not leave till they left him. I heard them say they would go and have something to drink; I ran up, and said,
"Gentlemen, you cannot go in here, there is nobody there; master is not there;" the booth was pinned down. One of them was on horseback.
Q. How soon did you give information at any place about this - A. About four o'clock in the morning. There were twelve or fourteen booths on the beath, but I was afraid to move to give information. It might last altogether about an hour and a half, but I was not there at the beginning. I was there for three-quarters of an hour, and had drank nothing that day but about a pint and a half of beer; and a gentleman, whose horse I was minding, gave me two glasses of stout.
Q. When you heard the cry of murder, why not go and give information, instead of going to the place - A. I went to see what was the matter, and when I got up I was afraid to leave: I said nothing to them - they went down the course, and I after him.
COURT. Q. Were you in the slightest degree intoxicated - A. I was not; I was frightened.
JAMES BESWICK. I am a Bow-street patrol. On the 14th, I apprehend the prisoner at Monlsey Hurst - I told him it was for ill using and robbing Sheehan; he at first said he was not there, and then that he was there; I put him into the cage at Hampton-court, and took him before Mr. Trimmer, at Heston, next day. The prosecutor saw him that evening; he (Sheehan) was at that time very lame; indeed, I found him at the workhouse - he could just put his foot to the ground, but could not walk without a stick; he was examined before the Magistrate, and the prisoner committed.
Cross-examined by MR. PLATT. Q. He at first said he was not there, and then that he was - A. Yes; I had old him of the robbery.
Q. He said he was not at the robbery - A. No; he said he was not on the heath at all, and then said that he was; three more persons were taken, but could not be identified.
JURY to SHEEHAN. Q. Do you know the man who was drinking with you - A. Yes; his name is Evans; he works with me; he went away about four minutes before this began.
Prisoner's Defence. I am innocent; the officer asked me, as we went along, if I knew any of the people who were there; I said I knew nothing of them, but should know them if I saw them again, and that I had heard two men in the morning say, that two of them worked in a brickfield.
One witness gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 18.
[trialAccount t18240916-280]
1517. GEORGE HAYNES was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Blankford, about six o'clock in the forenoon of the 23d of August, ( Ann Floyer and others being therein), and stealing therein two gowns, value 15 s; a shawl, value 10 s.; a bonnet, value 18 s., and a hat, value 18 s., the goods of Ann Floyer.
ANN FLOYER. I am a widow, and lodge at John Langford 's, Little Earl-street, Seven-dials. I have the second floor front room. On the the 23d of August, about five o'clock in the morning, I awoke, and these things were then safe in my room; I fell asleep, and awoke again at seven, and they were gone; my daughter was in bed with me: my son goes out at half-past five, and he unlocked the door - the house is all let out; the prisoner is a stranger.
WILLIAM HENRY LLOYD. I am a pawnbroker. On the the 24th of August, between three and four o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner came to pawn a gown; but having had information, I stopped it; he ran out, leaving it behind, but a watchman was coming by, and stopped him; and in about three minutes a duplicate was brought to me belonging to Graham and Co.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not ask you to let me go out, as the woman was outside who gave it to me to pawn - A. No; he said it belonged to his wife, who lived in Daker-street: I said I should go to Daker-street, and he immediately ran out.
ANN FLOYER. I live with my mother, and was asleep when this happened; one gown hung by the bed-side, the other was on the table - and the other things about the room.
LEONARD NEEDES. I am shopman to Graham and Co. On the 24th of August the prisoner pawned a gown for 3 s. 6 d. about half-past three o'clock in the afternoon, in the name of John Harris.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not come in twice about it - A. He came in first and asked 4 s. on it; I said I could lend but 3 s. 6 d., which he refused, but came in two or three minutes, and said he would take it.
BENJAMIN TIMBRELL. I am a constable. I took him into custody at Lloyd's.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was in the neighbourhood of Seven-dials; a woman who was frequently in company with me, came and asked where I was going; I said to Westminster; she said she was going that way, and in Tothill-street she asked me to pawn the gown for 4 s. - they would lend but 3 s. 6 d.; she told me to return and take it, and to pawn it in the name of Harris - she gave me sixpence for my pains; she offered me 6 d. more to pawn the other; I said I hoped she had come honestly by them - she said I might depend upon that; I asked her to come with me, but she would not; I said she was at the door, and would fetch her; two men followed me; I desired them to follow behind, that she should not go away; but she was gone.
- LLOYD. I saw nobody waiting outside.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Of stealing to the amount of 39 s. only, but not of breaking and entering.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18240916-281]
NEW COURT. (8th Day.)
Middlesex Cases, Fourth Jury, Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1511. GEORGE RICHARDS and JOHN MARTIN were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Mary Treacher, widow, about two o'clock on the night of the 18th of September, at Fulham, with intent to steal the goods and chattles therein being.
MARY TREACHER. I live in Vale-place, Hammersmith, in the parish of Fulham. On the night of Saturday last, I retired to bed about ten o'clock. I have no doubt but that all the doors were fastened; my daughter resides in the house with me, and no one else; I think it was my daughter who closed the back door; I fastened the stair-case window myself before I went to bed; my daughter went to bed at the same time. I found the door of my house broken the next morning. After I was in bed I was alarmed by a noise below, like a person attempting the house; I listened again, and heard a noise such as I could imagine might be that of a door being attempted to be wrenched, upon which I sprung a rattle. I think it might be after two o'clock, it was very foggy; I could see the lamp a light.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you any other name but Mary - A. No. I closed only the staircase window; I have no servant, we have a young person who comes to assist in the house to do the domestic business, she came that evening and brought some oysters.
MARY TREACHER. I am the daughter of the last witness I remember retiring to rest last Saturday night; I fastened the door which was broken open, and I believe the others door were not open when I went to bed. I was alarmed in the night; we had no property taken away. I was present last night when a dark lantern was found near the front door, it was about six o'clock; I saw part of a centre bit near the dark lantern, and part of it was found in the garden, it was all iron; I saw the pieces put together there was a pannel taken out of the coach door, this is the pannel, (looking at it.)
Cross-examined. Q. There is a servant who comes occasionally to the house - A. She came about eight o'clock that night, and went away directly. The door which was broken had been locked, barred, and bolted; I did it myself, and I am almost positive that I fastened the other door; I am positive all the doors were fastened when we went to bed.
THOMAS JENKINS. I am the watchman. In consequence of some alarm while I was crying the hour of three o'clock; I came to this house, and Low came with me; I went to the back of the house, got a ladder, and got over the wall; when I came to the back door of the house, I tried, and found it open; I heard somebody walking in the passage, I asked if that was Mr. Treacher, a man's voice answered Yes, I then went forward and saw a man, I said
"You are the man I want," he went up stairs and jumped out of the staircase window; I returned to the back door, thinking he was gone into the garden, but he had gone into the next garden; I went after him but he escaped; I heard some other persons in the house, but could not see them. I am sure there was more than one; I afterwards went round to the front of the house. I took Martin about six o'clock the same morning on the road going towards town, about sixty yards from the house, he had no hat on; I said,
"Have you lost your hat," he said
"Yes, he had been in an affray about three hours before, and lost it;" I took him to the watch-house, he did not say where the affray was. I went back to the house as soon as the patrol had taken Richards to the watch-house, it might be then about four o'clock, the thieves were gone. I found the front door open, and this dark lantern near it in the passage; this part of a centre bit there, and another part of it in the back garden; a crow bar was in the stair-case window. I found two hats in the passage, and a shoe on the landing place; one hat I have brought, the other I gave to Martin, as he came to town, because it rained; he claimed it as his own, at Queen-square. I believe what he said was taken in writing.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it you who addressed this young man about where he was going - A. Yes, he was about sixty yards from the house; he said he had been in an affray, and lost his hat - he openly owned it; it was then day-light. I was crying three o'clock when I was first called; it was last Saturday morning, and was very foggy. I do not know that I should know the features of a man ten yards from me.
JAMES PRICE. In consequence of some alarm, I came to the house about three o'clock. I went to the back of the house, and also to the front of it. I saw Richards and some other man come out of the front door. I got over the rails, between the two persons, and secured Richards. I asked him if he knew the others, he said that it was all right. I went back to the premises - went in at the front door, and through to the back door. I saw they had broken the door with a crow-bar. I tried the bar, and it corresponded exactly with the mark in the door.
Cross-examined. Q. Which did you apprehend - A. Richards. I do not know that I had ever seen the other man before, but I have seen him since. I was half a minute securing Richards, and the other ran away instantly - I could not swear to his person.
JOSEPH LAW. I am a shoemaker. I went to the house about three o'clock. I saw Price the patrole there. I wasat the front of the house when the two men came out - I saw Price take one. I ran after the other across the road - he got into a ditch close to the hedge; he then got out of the ditch and ran. I picked up a stone and knocked his hat off. I took up the hat, and a phosphorus box was in it. Martin afterwards owned the hat. I carried it into the house, and put it into the passage. About six o'clock I found a handkerchief against the street-door, outside - Martin claimed that also at Queen-square, before the Justice, while I was there. I was present when Martin was taken, close against the Hand and Flowers public-house. Jenkins and myself went across to him - Jenkins asked him where he had been, and what he had done with his hat - he said he had lost his hat in an affray at a lady's house - he then went to the watch-house, he had not any hat on at that time.
Cross-examined. Q. Did Jenkins hear him say this - A. Yes; I am sure he said he lost his hat at a lady's house - he spoke loud enough for Jenkins to hear him; he is one of the men who I pursued. I am certain, I saw him by the gas-light, and knew him directly I saw him in the morning. When he was running from me his back was to me - I had observed him for about five minutes; he ran across the road, about twenty or thirty yards. I picked up a stone to knock him down. I saw his face by the gas - he came full in my face coming over the pales.
Four witnesses gave Richards a good character; and one deposed the same for Martin.
RICHARDS - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 19.
MARTIN - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 17.

[trialAccount t18240916-284]
1521. WILLIAM BROWN was indicted for the wilful murder of Robert Scott .
SAMUEL HOLMES. I live at Mr. Reynold's brewery, West-street, Somer's-town. I have known the prisoner twelvemonths - he was our dray-clerk. I know Robert Scott, he worked in doors. I saw the prisoner in the storehouse, about half-past seven o'clock in the evening; Scott was coming up the yard; the prisoner asked Richard Jones if he had got any hung-cloth; he said he had not, but he had an apron. Scott then said,
"I have got an apron;" the prisoner who had a knife in his hand to prepare hung-cloth with, came out of the store-house, went past Jones up to Robert Scott, and without saying a word, took hold of his apron and cut it nearly across - he then cut a second time, and the deceased threw his apron back, to prevent his cutting it; the deceased then took hold of the prisoner's white apron, set his foot upon it and tore it; nothing further happened at that moment. I turned my head aside, and heard a witness say
"Good God Brown, what have you done." I turned round, and Scott was shaking his left hand, which held very much indeed, Scott turned to Brown, and said
"If you have any malice against me, don't cut me, strike me;" he then hit Brown with his right hand. Jones said to Scott
"Put your hand into the liquor-stand" - he did so. I said to him
"Take your hand out and go to a doctor;" we took up the bits of apron and tied up his hand; the prisoner with others went with him to the doctor's.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. The prisoner went with him to the doctor - A. Yes; there was no expression used but what I have stated; he cut the deceased's apron for a piece of bung-cloth. I did not see how the wound was inflicted; the aprons are made of the same cloths as hung-cloth.
RICHARD JONES. I am in the service of Mr. Reynolds. On the 9th of August, the prisoner asked me if I had any bung-cloth. I said No; Scott was coming up the yard, and said
"What do you want, bung-cloth?" he said Yes; then said he
"Cut Jones's apron," Brown said
"I may as well cut yours;" he passed by me and cut off a piece of Scott's apron with the knife. Scott did not seem to like it; he laid hold of Brown's apron and tore it off. Brown looked very savage, and took hold of Scott's apron again. Scott went back that he should not get his apron. Brown cut at the apron again, and cut Scott's hand - some of the blood came in my face. Scott said
"If you have any animosity against me, don't cut me, strike me;" he then struck Brown with his fist. I said
"For God's sake take him to a doctor," and Brown and Henniugton took him; they had had a few words a little time before. Brown was fond of giving a joke, but could not take one - they had a few jokes together. Brown said he did not mind me or my partner; but he would not put up with Scott's jokes, and would serve him out; but whether he meant to give him a thumping or not I do not know.
Cross-examined. A. Did he cut more than once at the apron - A. Yes; he cut one piece off at one chop, and then cut properly at the apron all the way.
THOMAS HILL. I am a drayman in the employ of Mr. Reynolds. I saw Scott lay hold of Brown's apron and tear it to pieces under his feet - after which Brown took hold of his apron, cut at it several times with the knife; the deceased, in attempting to pull it back from Brown, snatched at it, and received the cut. I saw Scott strike Brown - it was not a very severe blow.
Cross-examined. Q. In endeavouring to pull the apron back was the time he received the cut - A. I think it was the knife went against his hand. In my opinion it was quite an accident.
HENRY HAMMOND. Robert Scott was brought to me at the Middlesex Hospital about eight o'clock on the 9th of August. He died of a disease in the chest in about three weeks after he came. I have no doubt that the wound of his hand hastened his death. He died of inflamation of the lungs. He was opened, and the state of his lungs lead me to say this - there was an appearance of disease of the lungs before he received the wound; it was most likely excited by the wound, which otherwise might have remained quiet. The loss of blood was very considerable, which, with the irritation of the constitution, no doubt excited the complaint. I believe he would have died of the disease eventually, but he might have lived several years. I cannot say that the lungs might not have been excited by anything else - a cold, or other things, might have done it.
NOT GUILTY.
